From one-dimensional diffusion processes metastable behaviour to parabolic equations asymptotics.
Claudio Landim, and Christian Maura

Abstract
. Consider the one-dimensional elliptic operator given by ( ℒ ϵ ​ f ) ​ ( x ) = b ​ ( x ) ​ f ′ ​ ( x ) + ϵ ​ a ​ ( x ) ​ f ′′ ​ ( x ) , ({\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}f)(x)\;=\;{\textsf{b}}(x)\,f^{\prime}(x)\,+\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}(x)\,f^{\prime\prime}(x)\;, where the drift b : ℝ → ℝ {\textsf{b}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}} and the diffusion coefficient a : ℝ → ℝ {\textsf{a}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}} are periodic C 1 ​ ( ℝ ) C^{1}({\mathbb{R}}) functions satisfying further conditions, and ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 . Consider the initial-valued problem { ∂ t u ϵ = ℒ ϵ ​ u ϵ , u ϵ ​ ( 0 , ⋅ ) = u 0 ​ ( ⋅ ) , \left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\,u_{\epsilon}\,=\,\mathscr{L}_{\epsilon}\,u_{\epsilon}\;,\\ &u_{\epsilon}(0,\,\cdot)=u_{0}(\cdot)\;,\end{aligned}\right. for some bounded continuous function u 0 u_{0} . We prove the existence of time-scales θ ϵ ( 1 ) , … , θ ϵ ( 𝔮 ) \theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)},\,\dots,\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(\mathfrak{q})} such that θ ϵ ( 1 ) → ∞ \theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\to\infty , θ ϵ ( p + 1 ) / θ ϵ ( p ) → ∞ \theta_{\epsilon}^{(p+1)}/\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\to\infty , 1 ≤ p ≤ 𝔮 − 1 1\leq p\leq\mathfrak{q}-1 , probability measures p ⁡ ( x , ⋅ ) p(x,\cdot) , x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} , and kernels R t ( p ) ​ ( m j , m k ) R_{t}^{(p)}(m_{j},m_{k}) , where { m j : j ∈ ℤ } \{m_{j}:j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\} represents the set of stable equilibrium of the ODE 𝚡 ˙ ​ ( t ) = b ​ ( 𝚡 ​ ( t ) ) \dot{{\mathtt{x}}}(t)={\textsf{b}}({\mathtt{x}}(t)) such that lim ϵ → 0 u ϵ ​ ( t ​ θ ϵ ( p ) , x ) = ∑ j , k ∈ ℤ p ⁡ ( x , m j ) ​ R t ( p ) ​ ( m j , m k ) ​ u 0 ​ ( m k ) , \lim_{\epsilon\to 0}u_{\epsilon}(t\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)},x)\;=\;\sum_{j,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p(x,m_{j})\,R_{t}^{(p)}(m_{j},m_{k})\,u_{0}(m_{k})\;, for all t > 0 t>0 and x ∈ ℝ x\in{\mathbb{R}} . The solution u ϵ u_{\epsilon} asymptotic behavior description is completed by the characterisation of its behaviour in the intermediate time-scales ϱ ϵ \varrho_{\epsilon} such that ϱ ϵ / θ ϵ ( p ) → ∞ \varrho_{\epsilon}/\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\to\infty , ϱ ϵ / θ ϵ ( p + 1 ) → 0 \varrho_{\epsilon}/\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p+1)}\to 0 for some 0 ≤ p ≤ 𝔮 0\leq p\leq\mathfrak{q} , where θ ϵ ( 0 ) = 1 \theta_{\epsilon}^{(0)}=1 , θ ϵ ( 𝔮 + 1 ) = + ∞ \theta_{\epsilon}^{(\mathfrak{q}+1)}=+\infty . The proof relies on the analysis of the diffusion X ϵ ​ ( ⋅ ) X_{\epsilon}(\cdot) induced by the generator ℒ ϵ {\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon} based on the resolvent approach to metastability introduced in [ 21 ] .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary
60J60, Secondary
82C31.

1.
Introduction

Fix
\epsilon>0
, and consider the one-dimensional elliptic operator

{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}f}\;:=\;{\textsf{b}}\,\partial_{x}f\,+\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}\,\partial^{2}_{x}f\;.

Denote by
C^{k}({\mathbb{R}})
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the functions

f\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with
k
continuous derivatives. We assume
that the drift
{\textsf{b}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and the diffusion
coefficient
{\textsf{a}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are periodic with, say,
period
1
. We suppose that
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
are of
class
C^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
, and that their first derivatives are Lipschitz
continuous. We assume furthermore that the diffusion coefficient

{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
is strictly positive:
{\textsf{a}}(x)\geq c_{0}>0
, and that
in the interval
[0,1)
the drift
b
vanishes at a finite number
of points represented by
x_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq q
, and that

{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(x_{j})\neq 0
. This later condition and the periodicity of

{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
imply that the number of such points has to be even:

q=2N
for some
N\geq 1
.

Denote by
{\mathcal{M}}
,
{\mathcal{W}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
the stable, unstable
equilibrium points of the ODE
\dot{x}(t)={\textsf{b}}(x(t))
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{M}}}\,:=\,\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:{\textsf{b}}(x)=0\;,\;{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(x)<0\}\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{W}}}\,:=\,\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:{\textsf{b}}(x)=0\;,\;{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(x)>0\}\;.

(1.1)

Enumerate the elements in
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{W}}
as

{\mathcal{M}}=\{m_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
,
{\mathcal{W}}=\{\sigma_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
,
and assume, without loss of generality, that

\sigma_{k}\,<\,m_{k}\,<\,\sigma_{k+1}\;,\quad 0<\sigma_{0}<m_{0}<\dots<\sigma_{N-1}<m_{N-1}<1\;.

(1.2)

Fix a bounded, continuous function
u_{0}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and
consider the initial-valued problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u_{\epsilon}\,=\,{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\;,\\
&u_{\epsilon}(0,\cdot)=u_{0}(\cdot)\;.\end{aligned}\right.

(1.3)

In this article, we investigate the asymptotic behavior of the
solution
u_{\epsilon}(t,x)
of this equation.

The first main result of the article states that there exists a time-scale

\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}
,
\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\to\infty
, such that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}u_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon},x)\;=\;u_{0}(m_{k})

for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
x\in(\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k+1})
and time-scale

\varrho_{\epsilon}
such that
\varrho_{\epsilon}\to+\infty
,

\varrho_{\epsilon}/\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\to 0
. Moreover, there
exists a Markov semigroup
(p_{t}:t\geq 0)
defined on

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{M}}_{0}}:=\{\{m\}:m\in{\mathcal{M}}\}
such that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}u_{\epsilon}(s\,\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon},x)\;=\;\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p_{s}(\{m_{j}\},\{m_{k}\})\,u_{0}(m_{k})

for all
s>0
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
x\in(\sigma_{j},\sigma_{j+1})
. Hence,
the solution does not change until the time-scale

\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}
, when it start to evolve according to the
semigroup
(p_{t}:t\geq 0)
. This analysis is extended to longer
time-scales and provides a full description of the solution

u_{\epsilon}
asymptotic behavior.

We present an inductive procedure which provides

(a)

A sequence of time-scales
\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}
,

1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, such that

\theta^{(p+1)}_{\epsilon}/\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\to\infty
,

1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
, and
\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}=\theta_{\epsilon}
.

(b)

A sequence of partitions

\{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j),j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\cup\{{\mathscr{T}}_{p}\}
of
{\mathcal{M}}
,

1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, where
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(j)=\{m_{j}\}
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

{\mathscr{T}}_{1}=\varnothing
.

(c)

A sequence of non-degenerate
{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
-valued
continuous-time Markov semigroups
(p^{(p)}_{t}:t\geq 0)
. Here,

{\mathscr{S}}_{p}=\{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j):j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
,

p^{(1)}_{s}(\cdot,\cdot)=p_{s}(\cdot,\cdot)
, and non-degenerate
means that there exists
k\neq j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

p^{(p)}_{t}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))>0
for some (and therefore all)

t>0
.

The partitions are constructed inductively as follows. Assume that the
construction has been carried out up to layer
p
. Denote by

\{{\mathfrak{R}}^{(p)}_{k}:k\in K\}
the closed irreducible classes of the
Markov chain induced by the semigroup
(p^{(p)}_{t}:t\geq 0)
. Then, up
to relabelling to order the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(j)
,

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)\,=\,\bigcup_{i:{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(i)\in{\mathfrak{R}}^{(p)}_{k}}{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(i)\;.

The construction ends after a finite number of steps: the Markov chain
induced by the semigroup
(p^{({\mathfrak{q}})}_{t}:t\geq 0)
has only one closed
irreducible class or only transient states.

The previous construction determines a tree with
{\mathfrak{q}}

generations. Each element of the tree is a subset of
{\mathcal{M}}
. The
leaves are the sets
\{m_{j}\}
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Each parent is the union
of its children and corresponds to a closed irreducible class of a
Markov chain. The root represents the unique closed irreducible class
of the last Markov chain or the union of its transient states.

The second main result of the article states that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,u_{\epsilon}(\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}t\,,\,x)\ =\ \sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p_{t}^{(p)}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\sum_{m^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}\frac{\pi(m^{\prime})}{\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}\,u_{0}(m^{\prime})\ .

(1.4)

for all
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
t>0
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. In this formula,

{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))
has to be understood as the (asymptotic)
probability that the diffusion induced by the generator

{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
and starting from
x
hits the set

\cup_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(i)
at
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j)
, and

\pi(m^{\prime})/\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
as the proportion of time this diffusion
spends close to
m^{\prime}
when it visits the set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
.
We provide in (
2.27
) and (
2.25
) explicit formulas for

{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,\cdot)
,
\pi(\cdot)
, respectively, as functions of

{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
.

For any intermediate scale
\varrho_{\epsilon}
such that

\varrho_{\epsilon}/\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\to+\infty
,

\varrho_{\epsilon}/\theta^{(p+1)}_{\epsilon}\to 0
,
1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,u_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon}\,,\,x)\ =\ \sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k))\,\sum_{m^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)}\frac{\pi(m^{\prime})}{\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k))}\,u_{0}(m^{\prime})\ .

(1.5)

for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. The right-hand side can be obtained from the
right-hand side of (
1.4
) by letting
t\to\infty
. A similar
result can be derived for a time-scale
\varrho_{\epsilon}
such that

\varrho_{\epsilon}/\theta^{({\mathfrak{q}})}_{\epsilon}\to+\infty
, but it
requires the initial condition
u_{0}(\cdot)
to be periodic.

Resolvent approach to metastability

The proof is based on the stochastic representation of the solution

u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot)
to (
1.3
) in terms of the diffusion

X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
induced by the generator
{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
.

The Freidlin and Wentzell
[
11
]
large deviations theory for
diffusions permit to characterise the asymptotic behaviour of the
solution
u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot)
in the intermediate time-scales

(\varrho_{\epsilon}:\epsilon>0)
such that

\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\prec\varrho_{\epsilon}\prec\theta^{(p+1)}_{\epsilon}
,
1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
,
[
9
,
10
,
16
]
. Here and below, for two positive sequences
\lambda_{\epsilon}

and
\mu_{\epsilon}
, write

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lambda_{\epsilon}\prec\mu_{\epsilon}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mu_{\epsilon}\succ\lambda_{\epsilon}}
if

\lambda_{\epsilon}/\mu_{\epsilon}\to 0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.
Similar results were obtained in
[
14
,
15
]
with purely
analytical methods and in
[
28
]
with stochastic analysis
tools.

More recently, sharp estimates for the transition time between two
wells, the so-called Eyring-Kramers formula, have been obtained for
diffusion processes
[
2
,
22
,
24
]
based on potential theory

[
17
]
. These results leaded to the description of the
evolution of small perturbations of dynamical systems between the
deepest wells, the so-called metastable behavior
[
29
,
25
]
. Similar results were obtained with analytical techniques,

[
26
,
1
]
and references therein.

We follow here the resolvent approach to metastability
[
20
,
21
]
. It consists in showing first that the solution of a resolvent
equation is asymptotically constant on each well. More precisely,
denote by
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the wells of the
diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
observed at the time-scale

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}
. Fix
\lambda>0
, a function

{\mathfrak{g}}\colon{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
\phi_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
be the
solution of the resolvent equation

(\lambda-\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\,{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon})\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}\,=\,G\,,

(1.6)

where

G(x)\,=\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathfrak{g}}(k)\,\chi_{{}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}}(x)\;,

(1.7)

and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\chi_{{}_{{\mathscr{A}}}}
,
{\mathscr{A}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, represents
the indicator function of the set
{\mathscr{A}}
. Theorem
3.3
asserts
that on each well
{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))

\phi_{\epsilon}(\cdot)

converges, as
\epsilon\to 0
, to
{\mathfrak{f}}(k)
, where
{\mathfrak{f}}
is the
solution of the reduced resolvent equation

(\lambda-{\mathfrak{L}}_{p})\,{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{g}}\,,

where
{\mathfrak{L}}_{p}
is the generator of a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-valued Markov chain
{\mathfrak{X}}_{p}
.

From this result, one deduces in Theorem
3.2
that probability
of the event
\{X_{\epsilon}(t\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)})\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\}
converges to the probability that
{\mathfrak{X}}_{p}(t)=k
for
all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
t>0
. From this convergence and the stochastic
representation of the solution of linear parabolic equation, one
completes the proof of (
1.4
), (
1.5
).

This approach has been successfully applied in
[
18
,
19
]
to
describe the asymptotic behavior at the critical time-scales of the
linear parabolic equations solution of

\partial_{t}u_{\epsilon}\,={\mathscr{L}}^{V}_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\;,\quad{\mathscr{L}}^{V}_{\epsilon}f\,=\,\epsilon\,\Delta f\,-\,(\nabla V+\ell)\,\nabla f\,,

where
V
is a Morse potential and
\ell
a divergence free field
orthogonal to
V
. The conditions on the field
\ell
are necessary
and sufficient for the measure
Z_{\epsilon}^{-1}\exp\{-V(x)/\epsilon\}\,dx
to be stationary for the diffusion process induced by the generator

{\mathscr{L}}^{V}_{\epsilon}

[
24
]
.

In this article, we investigate the one-dimensional version of this
problem, allowing the diffusion coefficient
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
and the
drift
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
to be any periodic
C^{1}
function whose
derivative is Lipschitz continuous. One of the main obstacles lies in
the fact that the stationary state is not explicitly known, preventing
computation of capacities. In
[
23
]
, for instance, assuming that

{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
is constant, based on an explicit formula for the
quasi-potential derived in
[
8
]
, the evolution of the diffusion
among the deepest wells was obtained from the computation of the
capacities.

It turns out that the proof does not rely deeply on the periodicity of
the parameters
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
. It is plausible that
the random coefficients case
[
4
,
5
]
can be handled with
some adaptations, or their discrete versions
[
3
]
. The
degenerate case in which the first
2n
,
n\geq 1
, derivatives of the
drift
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
vanish at the points where
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)

vanishes seems also possible to tackle.

The article is organised as follows. The main results are presented in
Section
2
. In Section
3
, we introduce the
one-dimensional diffusion process
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
and state the
results describing its asymptotic behavior at the critical
time-scales. In Section
4
, we present some estimates on
hitting times needed throughout the paper. This section can be skipped
at a first reading. In Sections
5
and
6
, we derive
the asymptotic behavior of the resolvent equation solution in the
critical time-scales (Theorem
3.3
). In Section
7
, we
describe the metastable behavior of the diffusion at the critical
time-scales, and we establish the finite-dimensional distributions
convergence. In Section
8
, we prove Theorems
2.1
and

2.5
. In the first appendix, we present properties of the
hierarchical structure introduced in Section
2
, and used
throughout the paper. In Appendix
B
, we present some
properties of elliptic equations weak solutions needed in the previous
sections.

2.
Notation and Results

In this section, we state the main results of the article. Denote by

S\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
the potential defined by

S(x)\;=\;-\,\int_{0}^{x}\frac{{\textsf{b}}(z)}{{\textsf{a}}(z)}\,dz\;.

(2.1)

Let
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, be the sets
defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)}\,:=\,\{m_{k}\}\;,\;\;\text{and set}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{S}}_{1}}:=\{{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k):k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\;.

The first time-scale encodes the size of the minimal energy barrier
between
S
-local minima. Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{+}_{k}}:=S(\sigma_{k+1})-S(m_{k}),\hskip 28.45274pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{-}_{k}}:=S(\sigma_{k})-S(m_{k}),

(2.2)

which can be regarded as escape barriers on the right and left of the
set
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
, respectively. Clearly, as
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
are periodic functions,

h^{\pm}_{k+N}\,=\,h^{\pm}_{k}\quad\text{for all}\;\;k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;.

(2.3)

For each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
define the height (of the escape barrier) of

{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
as the minimum of its right and left escape
barriers. Let
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
be the smallest height, and

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}
the associated first time-scale:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h_{k}}\,:=\,\min\{h^{-}_{k},h^{+}_{k}\}\;,\;k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;,\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}}\,:=\,\min\{h_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}\;,\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}}\,:=\,e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}/\epsilon}\;.

(2.4)

By (
2.3
),
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}=\min\{h_{k}:0\leq k<N\}
, so that

{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}>0
.

For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{1}(k)\,:=\,\pi(\{m_{k}\})}\,=\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(m_{k})}\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{S^{\prime\prime}(m_{k})}}\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{-\,2\pi}{{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(m_{k})\,{\textsf{a}}(m_{k})}}\;,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}(k,k+1)}\,:=\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})}}\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi\,{\textsf{a}}(\sigma_{k+1})}{{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})}}\;\cdot\end{gathered}

(2.5)

Here, the indices
k
,
k+1
of
\sigma_{1}(k,k+1)
indicate that we
consider the
S
-local maximum between the local minima
m_{k}
and

m_{k+1}
. Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{1}(k+1,k)=\sigma_{1}(k,k+1)
.
Define the jump rates

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k\pm 1))}\;:=\;\frac{1}{\pi_{1}(k)\,\sigma_{1}(k,k\pm 1)}\;{\mathbf{1}}\{h^{\pm}_{k}={\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\}\;.

(2.6)

Therefore, the jump rate from
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
to
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k\pm 1)

vanishes if
h^{\pm}_{k}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
. [By definition

{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\leq h^{\pm}_{j}
for all
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.] Denote by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{X}}_{1}(\cdot)
the
{\mathscr{S}}_{1}
-valued Markov chain
induced by the generator
\mathbb{L}_{1}
defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({\mathbb{L}}_{1}f)({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))}\,:=\,\sum_{a=\pm 1}R_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+a))\,\big[\,f({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+a))-f({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,\big]\;,\quad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;.

(2.7)

The first main result of this article reads as follows. Denote by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathcal{D}}(m)
,
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, the domain of attraction of
m

for the ODE
\dot{x}(t)={\textsf{b}}(x(t))
:

{\mathcal{D}}(m_{k})=(\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k+1})
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. For

x\in{\mathcal{D}}(m_{j})
, let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{h}}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))}=\delta_{j,k}
, where

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{j,k}
represents Kronecker’s delta, while for

x=\sigma_{j}
,

{\mathtt{h}}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))=(1/2)\delta_{k,j}+(1/2)\delta_{k,j-1}
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Fix a bounded continuous function
u_{0}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Denote
by
u_{\epsilon}
the solution of the parabolic equation
(
1.3
). Then,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}u_{\epsilon}(t\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)},x)\;=\;\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathtt{h}}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(j))\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p_{t}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,u_{0}(m_{k})\;,

for all
t>0
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. In this formula,

p_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
represents the transition probability of the
Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}(\cdot)
. Moreover, for any sequence

1\prec\varrho_{\epsilon}\prec\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}u_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon},x)\;=\;u_{0}(m)\;,

for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(m)
, and for any sequence

\epsilon^{-1}\prec\varrho_{\epsilon}\prec\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}u_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon},\sigma_{j})\;=\;(1/2)\,u_{0}(m_{j-1})\,+\,(1/2)\,u_{0}(m_{j})\;,

for all
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Longer time-scales

In this subsection, we present a recursive method to derive the
higher-order metastable structure of the process
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
.
At each iteration
q\geq 1
, the inductive procedure yields a triplet

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Gamma_{q}:=({\mathcal{P}}_{q}\,,\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}\,,\,{\mathbb{L}}_{q})},

(2.8)

where
{\mathcal{P}}_{q}:={\mathscr{S}}_{q}\cup\{{\mathscr{T}}_{q}\}
is a partition of the
set
{\mathcal{M}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{S}}_{q}}:=\{{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j):j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
,

{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}>0
is an energy barrier, and
{\mathbb{L}}_{q}
is the generator of a

{\mathscr{S}}_{q}
-valued Markov chain, denoted by
{\mathbb{X}}_{q}(\cdot)
.

In this construction, two subsets
A
,
B
of
{\mathbb{R}}
are said to be
equivalent,
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}A\sim B
, if
B=A+k
for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
where
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}A+k=\{x+k:x\in A\}
.

The first layer of this iterative construction has been carried out in
the previous section. The components of the triplet
\Gamma_{1}
are

{\mathcal{P}}_{1}={\mathscr{S}}_{1}=\{\{m_{j}\}:j\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}
,

{\mathscr{T}}_{1}=\varnothing
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
defined in (
2.4
), the Markov
chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}(\cdot)
induced by the generator
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
defined in
(
2.7
).

The recursive constructions of the triplets can be divided in three
stages. We start showing that the first layer
\Gamma_{1}
fullfils
conditions
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
—
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}
introduced below. Then, assuming
that the layer
\Gamma_{p}
satisfy these conditions and that there is
one or more
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-closed irreducible class, we define a new layer

\Gamma_{p+1}
. To complete the construction, we prove that the new
layer satisfy properties
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
—
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}
, and that the number
of
{\mathbb{X}}_{p+1}
-closed irreducible classes, up to equivalences, is
strictly smaller than the
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-closed irreducible classes. This
last property ensures that the recursive procedure ends after a finite
number of steps.

We start with the properties of the first layer. Recall the
definition of the
\Gamma_{1}
-components given in the previous
subsection. It is clear that the following conditions are satisfied
for
q=1
.

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(q)
​​​

] For every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)\neq\varnothing
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)\subset{\mathcal{M}}
.

Two subsets
C
,
D
of
{\mathbb{R}}
are said to be
well ordered
, if

C\cap D=\varnothing
and
x<y
or
x>y
for every
x\in C
and

y\in D
. In the first case this relation is represented by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C<D
, while in the second one it is denoted by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C>D
.

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(q)
​​​

]
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)<{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
for
j<k
,
and
m_{{\textsf{j}}_{q}}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(0)
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textsf{j}}_{q}}\,:=\,\min\{k\geq 0:m_{k}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}\}\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{M}}_{q}}:=\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)\;.

(2.9)

The integer
{\textsf{j}}_{q}
is introduced to enumerate the sets

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(i)
,
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The set
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(0)
will be the one which
contains
m_{{\textsf{j}}_{q}}
. Recall the equivalence relation
\sim

introduced above, and denote by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}\in{\mathbb{N}}
the
number of equivalent classes of the set
{\mathscr{S}}_{q}
, so that

{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}=N
.

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}(q)
​​​

]

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j+{\mathfrak{u}}_{q})={\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)+1
for all
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
In particular,
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j+{\mathfrak{u}}_{q})\sim{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(j)
.

Let
C
be a non empty subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
. We say that the elements in

C
have the same
depth
if
S(x)=S(y)
for all
x
,

y\in C
. For a set
A\subset{\mathbb{R}}
where all elements share the
same depth, let
S(A)
denote the common value of
S
at
A
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}S(A)}\,:=\,S(x),\;\;\forall\;x\in A.

(2.10)

Since the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
are singletons,

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{4}(q)
​​​

] For all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the local minima in

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
have the same
depth
.

Given two subsets
A
,
B
of
{\mathbb{R}}
such that
A<B
or
B<A
, define
the
barrier
between
A
and
B
as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Lambda(A,B)}\,:=\,\sup_{x\in[x^{+}_{A},x^{-}_{B}]}S(x)\;\;\text{if}\;\;A<B\quad\text{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Lambda(A,B)}\,:=\,\Lambda(B,A)\;\;\text{if}\;\;B<A\;.

(2.11)

In this formula,
x^{+}_{A}
is the rightmost element of the closure of
A
, and

x^{-}_{B}
is the leftmost element on the closure of
B
:

x^{+}_{A}:=\sup\{x\in A\}
,
x^{-}_{B}:=\inf\{x\in B\}
.

Condition
{\mathcal{P}}_{5}(1)
below is empty as the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
are
singletons. Conditions
{\mathcal{P}}_{6}(1)
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{7}(1)
follow from the
definition of the height
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
and the jump rates
R_{1}
.
Postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{5}(q)
states that the energetic barrier between the
elements of a set
{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
is bounded by
{\mathfrak{h}}_{q-1}
, and
postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{6}(q)
that the energetic barrier between the set

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
and its neighbours is larger than
{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}
. Postulate

{\mathcal{P}}_{7}(q)
asserts that the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{q}(\cdot)
jumps only
to nearest neighbour sets, and that the jump is possible only in the
energetic barrier is equal to
{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}
.

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{5}(q)
​​​

] For every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
for which

{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)
has two or more elements, and for every

m^{\prime},m^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k)

\Lambda(\{m^{\prime}\},\{m^{\prime\prime}\})-S({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))\,\leq\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{q-1}\;.

(2.12)

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{6}(q)
​​​

] For every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k\pm 1))-S({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))\geq{\mathfrak{h}}_{q}.

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{7}(q)
​​​

] For every
k
,
l\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(l))>0
if, and only if,
l=k\pm 1

and

\displaystyle\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k\pm 1))-S({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))={\mathfrak{h}}_{q}
.

We prove below that the next two conditions are fulfilled for
q=1
.

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{8}(q)
​​​

] Let
a=\pm 1
. If

R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+a))>0
for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, then

S({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))\geq S({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+a)).

Moreover, if
R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+a),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k))=0
, then the
previous inequality is strict.

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{9}(q)
​​​

]
The jump rates are
{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}
-periodic:

R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k+{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}\pm 1))=R_{q}({\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{q}(k\pm 1))
for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Condition
{\mathcal{P}}_{8}(1)
is easy to check. Assume that

R_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+1))>0
for some
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The
same argument applies to
k-1
. By (
2.2
), (
2.6
),

S(m_{k})=S(\sigma_{k})-h^{+}_{k}=S(\sigma_{k})-{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
. On the
other hand, by the definition (
2.4
) of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
and (
2.2
),

S(\sigma_{k})-{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\geq S(\sigma_{k})-h^{-}_{k+1}=S(m_{k+1})
,
so that
S(m_{k})\geq S(m_{k+1})
. If

R_{1}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+1),{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))=0
, then by (
2.6
),

h^{-}_{k+1}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
and the previous inequality is strict, which
proves the postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{8}(1)
.

Postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{9}(1)
follows from the definition (
2.6
) of the
jump rates
R_{1}
and from the
1
-periodicity of the functions

{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
.

Denote by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{n}}_{q}
the number of
{\mathbb{X}}_{q}
-closed
irreducible classes, up to equivalence. The proof of postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}(1)
is presented in Appendix
A
.

[
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}(q)
​​​

] Let
{\mathfrak{n}}_{0}=N
,
{\mathfrak{n}}_{q}<{\mathfrak{n}}_{q-1}
.

Recursive procedure

We may now start the recursive procedure, see Figure
1

below. Fix
p\geq 1
and assume that the construction of the triplets

\Gamma_{q}
,
1\leq q\leq p
, defined in (
2.8
), has been
carried out, and that each
\Gamma_{q}
satisfy the properties

{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(q)
–
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}(q)
introduced above. In this subsection,
we present a recursive scheme which provides a triplet
\Gamma_{p+1}

satisfying the same properties.

Denote by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}(k)
,
k\in K
, where
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}K

is a subset of
{\mathbb{N}}
, the closed irreducible classes of the Markov
chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
, and by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{T}}_{p}
the transient
states. If
K
is empty or a singleton, the construction is
completed. Otherwise, one additional layer is formed.

If
K
is empty,
{\mathfrak{n}}_{p}=0
. If
K
is a singleton, that is, if there
is only one closed irreducible class, by postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{7}(p)
, this
irreducible class has to be connected. By postulate
{\mathscr{P}}_{9}(p)
, it
cannot be bounded or semi-infinite. Hence, if there is only one
irreducibles class, it must be the all set
{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
. In this case, we
say that
{\mathfrak{n}}_{p}=0
.

Assume, from now, that
K
has at least two elements. By postulate

{\mathscr{P}}_{9}(p)
, if
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
has two distinct closed
irreducibles classes, it has a countable number of them, and
{\mathfrak{n}}_{p}\geq 1
. Hence, the construction ends if
{\mathfrak{n}}_{p}=0
and it
continues if
{\mathfrak{n}}_{p}\geq 1
.

Since
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
takes value in
{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
, there exist
disjoint subsets
I_{k}
of
{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}(k)=\{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(i):i\in I_{k}\}
. Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)}\,:=\,\bigcup_{i\colon{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(i)\in{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}(k)}{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(i)\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}}\,:=\,\bigcup_{k\in K}{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)\;.

(2.13)

Hence,
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}
contains all the
S
-local minima
m
belonging
to some
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-recurrent state
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}}:=\{{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k):k\in K\}
.
The number of equivalent classes of
{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}
, denoted by

{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}
, is, by construction, the number of
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-closed
irreducible classes, up to equivalence, which is represented by

{\mathfrak{n}}_{p}
. Thus
{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}={\mathfrak{n}}_{p}\geq 1
.

(
\alpha
)
Properties of the sets

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(i)
. By postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(p)
and by definition of
the sets
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)
,
j\in K
,
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}
, each set

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)
is not empty, and
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
.

By postulates
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(p)
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{7}(p)
, the sets

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
are ordered:

\text{For $j\neq k\in K$ either}\;\;{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)\,<\,{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)\;\;\text{or}\;\;{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)\,<\,{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)\;.

(2.14)

By postulates
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}(p)
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{9}(p)
, if
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}(k)
is a

{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-recurrent class, then so is
1+{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}(k)
. More precisely,

if
\{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j):j\in I\}
is a
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-recurrent
class for some set
I\subset K
, then
\{1+{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j):j\in I\}

(2.15)

is also a
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-recurrent class.

Claim A
: For each set

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
,
k\in K
, there exist sets
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)
,

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j^{\prime})
,
j
,
j^{\prime}\in K
, such that

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)\,<\,{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)\,<\,{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j^{\prime})
. In
particular, the set
K
is countably infinite, and each element

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
of
{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}
is bounded.

Indeed, fix a set
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
,
k\in K
. Since

{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}\geq 2
, the set
{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}
contains at least two
elements. Let
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)
,
j\neq k
, be another element of

{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}
. In view of (
2.14
), assume without loss of
generality that
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)<{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
. Choose
\ell

large enough so that
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)\not>\ell+{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)

[this means that
\ell+{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)
contains an element larger
than an element of
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
]. By (
2.15
),

\ell+{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)={\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j^{\prime})
for some
j^{\prime}\in K
.
Since the sets are ordered, and

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)\not>{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j^{\prime})
,

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)<{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j^{\prime})
, so that

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j)<{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)<{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(j^{\prime})
, as claimed.

Claim B
:
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}\cap\{m_{0},\dots,m_{N-1}\}\not=\varnothing
.

Since, by hypothesis, the set

{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}=\{{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k):k\in K\}
has two or more
equivalent classes, there exists a non-empty set
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
,

k\in K
. Let
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

[\,{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)+j\,]\cap[0,1)\neq\varnothing
. By
(
2.15
),
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)+j={\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k^{\prime})
for some

k^{\prime}\in K
. Thus,
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k^{\prime})\cap[0,1)\neq\varnothing
. Since
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k^{\prime})\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}
and, as
already proved,
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
,

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}\cap\{m_{0},\dots,m_{N-1}\}\not=\varnothing
, as
claimed.

(
\beta
)
Reordering the sets

{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(i)
. Recall the definition of
{\textsf{j}}_{p+1}

introduced (
2.9
). By Claim B,

{\textsf{j}}_{p+1}\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. Denote by
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(0)
the
sets
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
,
k\in K
, which contains
m_{{\textsf{j}}_{p+1}}
. By (
2.14
) and Claim A, we may relabel the
sets
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
,
k\in K
, to obtain an ordered family

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(i)
,
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfying postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(p+1)
. This family is indexed by
{\mathbb{Z}}
because, by Claim
A, for each element there is a smaller and larger one.

Since, as observed in Step (
\alpha)
, each set
{\mathscr{M}}^{*}_{p+1}(k)
,

k\in K
, is not empty, the same property holds for the sets

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(i)
,
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Moreover,

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(i)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
, showing that
postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(p+1)
holds. Hence, up to this point, we proved
that postulates
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(p+1)
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(p+1)
are in force. The next
result, as well as the following two, is proved in Appendix

A
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Fix
p\geq 1
and assume that the construction of the triplets

\Gamma_{q}
,
1\leq q\leq p
, defined in (
2.8
), has been
carried out, and that each
\Gamma_{q}
satisfy the properties

{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(q)
—
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}(q)
. Assume, furthermore, that

{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}\geq 2
. Then, postulates
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(p+1)
—
{\mathcal{P}}_{4}(p+1)

are in force.

(
\gamma
)
Construction of the
height

{\mathfrak{h}}_{p+1}
. By Proposition
2.2
and postulates

{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(p+1)
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{4}(p+1)
, the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)
,

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, are ordered and the elements of each set have the same
depth. We may therefore define the heights of the left and right
escape barriers of the set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)
as

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{p+1,\pm}_{k}}\,:=\,\Lambda\,\big({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k\pm 1)\,,\,{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)\big)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k))\;,\quad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;.\end{gathered}

(2.16)

Define the height
h^{p+1}_{k}
of the escape barrier of

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)
as the minimum of its left and right heights, and let

{\mathfrak{h}}_{p+1}
be the smallest
(p+1)
-order height:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h^{p+1}_{k}}:=h^{p+1,-}_{k}\wedge h^{p+1,+}_{k}\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{h}}_{p+1}}:=\min\big\{\,h^{p+1}_{k}:k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\,\big\}\;.

(2.17)

By postulates
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}(p+1)
, and the
1
-periodicity of the functions

{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)

h^{p+1}_{k+{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}}\,=\,h^{p+1}_{k}\;\;\text{for all}\;\;k\,\in\,{\mathbb{Z}}\;.

(2.18)

Hence, the heights
h^{p+1}_{k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, take at most

{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}
different values. Since
{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}={\mathfrak{n}}_{p}
, and,
by postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}(p)
,
{\mathfrak{n}}_{p}<N
,

{\mathfrak{h}}^{p+1}\in(0,\infty)
, and
{\mathfrak{h}}^{p+1}=h^{p+1}_{k}
for some

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
2.2
, postulates

{\mathcal{P}}_{5}(p+1)
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{6}(p+1)
are in force. Moreover,

{\mathfrak{h}}_{p+1}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}
.

(
\delta
)
Construction of the Markov chain

{\mathbb{X}}_{(p+1)}(\cdot)
. Denote by
m^{+}_{p+1,i}
,
m^{-}_{p+1,i}
,

i\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the rightmost, leftmost element of
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(i)
,
respectively:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m^{-}_{p+1,i}}\,:=\,\min\{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(i)\}\;,\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m^{+}_{p+1,i}}\,:=\,\max\{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(i)\}.

(2.19)

Let
{\mathscr{W}}^{(p+1)}_{k,k+1}
be the set of the absolute maximum of

S
on the intervals
[m^{+}_{p+1,k},m^{-}_{p+1,k+1}]
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{W}}^{(p+1)}_{k,k+1}}\;:=\;\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x\;\in\;[m^{+}_{p+1,k}\,,\,m^{-}_{p+1,k+1}]}S(x)\;.

(2.20)

Clearly, the elements of
{\mathscr{W}}^{(p+1)}_{k,k+1}
have the same
height and

S({\mathscr{W}}^{(p+1)}_{k,k+1})\,=\,\Lambda\big({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k+1)\big)\;.

Let the weight
\pi_{p+1}(k)
of the set of local minima

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)
and the weight
\sigma_{p+1}(k,k+1)
of the set of
maxima
{\mathscr{W}}^{(p+1)}_{k,k+1}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, be defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{p+1}(k)}\,:=\,\sum_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(m)}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{S^{\prime\prime}(m)}}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sigma_{p+1}(k,k+1)}\,:=\,\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathscr{W}}^{(p+1)}_{k,k+1}}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}}\;\cdot

(2.21)

Define the
(p+1)
-th-level jump rates as

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R_{p+1}({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k+1))}\;:=\;\frac{1}{\pi_{p+1}(k)\,\sigma_{p+1}(k,k+1)}\;{\mathbf{1}}\{\,h^{p+1,+}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p+1}\,\}\;,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R_{p+1}({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k-1))}\;:=\;\frac{1}{\pi_{p+1}(k)\,\sigma_{p+1}(k-1,k)}\;{\mathbf{1}}\{\,h^{p+1,-}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p+1}\,\}\;.\end{gathered}

(2.22)

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{X}}_{p+1}(\cdot)}
be the
{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}-
valued
Markov chain induced by the generator
{\mathbb{L}}_{p+1}
given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({\mathbb{L}}_{p+1}f)\,({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k))}\;=\;\sum_{a=\pm 1}R_{p+1}({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k+a))\,[\,f({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k+a))-f({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k))\,]\;,

(2.23)

for any
f\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)\in{\mathscr{S}}_{p+1}
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
2.2
, postulates

{\mathcal{P}}_{7}(p+1)
—
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}(p+1)
are in force. Moreover,
there exists
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

R_{p+1}({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k+1))\,+\,R_{p+1}({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k-1))\,>\,0\;.

(2.24)

Conclusion:

According to Propositions
2.2
–
2.4
, if triplets

\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{p}
,
p\geq 1
, have been constructed
fulfilling conditions
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}
—
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}
, and if

{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}={\mathfrak{n}}_{p}\geq 2
, then a new triplet
\Gamma_{p+1}
can
be constructed fulfilling conditions
{\mathcal{P}}_{1}(p+1)
—
{\mathcal{P}}_{10}(p+1)
. Moreover,
{\mathfrak{u}}_{1}=N
, the sequence

({\mathfrak{u}}_{q}:1\leq q\leq p+1)
is strictly decreasing, and the sequence

({\mathfrak{h}}_{q}:1\leq q\leq p+1)
is strictly increasing.

Since the sequence
{\mathfrak{u}}_{q}
starts from
N
and strictly decreases,
there exists
p\geq 1
such that
{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}=0
(ore, equivalently,

{\mathfrak{n}}_{p}=0
). Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{q}}}:=\min\{p\geq 1:{\mathfrak{u}}_{p+1}=0\}
. The
iterative construction ends with the triplet
\Gamma_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and the
recursive method produced the triplets
\Gamma_{r}
,
1\leq r\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
.
The last Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
has only transient states
or
{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is a closed irreducible class. We describe further
this last layer in the next subsection.

Figure 1.
Here we represent a hierarchy structure with 5 different
levels. For the level one, the states
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)
,
-2\leq k\leq 4

are given by
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k)=\{m_{k}\}
. Moreover,
{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(0)
and

{\mathscr{M}}_{1}(1)
belong to the same
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}(\cdot)-
recurrent class,
while the remaining states that appear in the figure are
absorbing. For the level 2, we have
{\mathscr{M}}_{2}(0)=\{m_{0},m_{1}\}
and

{\mathscr{M}}_{2}(k)=\{m_{k}\}
, for
k\in\{-2,-1,2,3,4\}
. At this level,

{\mathscr{M}}_{2}(0)
is a
{\mathbb{X}}_{2}(\cdot)-
transient state, while the
remaining states are absorbing. Turning to level 3, we see the first
relabeling. Here
{\textsf{j}}_{3}=2
and the metastable states of level 3
that appear in the figure are given by
{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(-2)=\{m_{-2}\}
,

{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(-1)=\{m_{-1}\}
,
{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(0)=\{m_{2}\}
,
{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(1)=\{m_{3}\}

and
{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(2)=\{m_{4}\}
. At this level,
{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(-2)
is

{\mathbb{X}}_{3}(\cdot)-
transient,
{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(-1)
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(2)
are
absorbing, and
\{{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(0),{\mathscr{M}}_{3}(1)\}
is a recurrent class. At
level 4 we have the states
{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(-1)=\{m_{-1}\}
,

{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(0)=\{m_{2},m_{3}\}
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(1)=\{m_{4}\}
. Here

{\textsf{j}}_{4}=2
. At this level, the states
{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(-1)
and

{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(1)
are absorbing, while the class

\{{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(0),{\mathscr{M}}_{4}(1)\}
is
{\mathbb{X}}_{4}(\cdot)-
recurrent. Finally,
at the level 5, the only states appearing in the figure are

{\mathscr{M}}_{5}(0)=\{m_{-1},\;m_{2},\;m_{3}\}
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{5}(1)=\{m_{4}\}
,
while
{\textsf{j}}_{5}=2
. Finally, as
{\mathfrak{h}}_{5}<h_{0}^{5,-}
, then

R_{5}({\mathscr{M}}_{5}(0),{\mathscr{M}}_{5}(1))>0,
while

R_{5}({\mathscr{M}}_{5}(0),{\mathscr{M}}_{)}5(-1))=0
. Similarly, as

{\mathfrak{h}}_{5}<h_{1}^{5,+}
, then
R_{5}({\mathscr{M}}_{5}(1),{\mathscr{M}}_{5}(0))>0,
while

R_{5}({\mathscr{M}}_{5}(1),{\mathscr{M}}_{5}(2))=0
.

The parabolic equation

The statement of the second main result of this article requires some
more notation. For
m\in\mathcal{M}
and finite subsets
{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}
of

{\mathcal{M}}
, let the weights
\pi(m)
,
\pi_{p+1}({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime})
be given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi(m_{k})}\,:=\,\pi_{1}(k)\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi({\mathscr{M}}^{\prime})}\,:=\,\sum_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}^{\prime}}\pi(m)\;.

(2.25)

Set also, for
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi(m)}:=\pi(m_{k})
if
m=m_{k}
.

For
2\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
{\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)
,
{\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)

the indices of the first set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j)
to the left, right of
x
,
respectively:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)}\,:=\,\max\{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\exists\;m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j)\,,\,m\leq x\,\}\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)}\,:=\,\min\{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\exists\;m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)\,,\,m\geq x\,\}\;.

(2.26)

Mind that either
{\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)={\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)
or

{\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)={\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)+1
.

Suppose that
{\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)<{\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)
. In this case,

m^{+}_{p,{\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)}<x<m^{-}_{p,{\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)}
, where the local
minima
m^{\pm}_{p,k}
have been introduced in (
2.19
). Let

m_{{\mathtt{l}}}=m^{+}_{p,{\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)}
,
m_{{\mathtt{r}}}=m^{-}_{p,{\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)}

to simplify notation.
Denote by
{\mathfrak{S}}_{p}(x)
the finite set where
S(\cdot)
attains its global
maximum in the interval
[m_{{\mathtt{l}}},m_{{\mathtt{r}}}]
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{S}}_{p}(x)}\,:=\,\big\{\sigma\in(m_{{\mathtt{l}}},m_{{\mathtt{r}}})\cap{\mathcal{W}}:S(\sigma)=\max_{y\in[m_{{\mathtt{l}}},m_{{\mathtt{r}}}]}S(y)\big\}\;,

and let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{p}(x)}:=\{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{p}(x):\sigma<x\}
. Mind that
{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{p}(x)
may be empty.

Let
{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,\cdot)
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, be the
probability measure on
{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
given by

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}\,=\,\delta_{k,{\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)}\quad\text{if ${\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)={\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)$ }\;,\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}({\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)))}\,:=\,1\,-\,{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}({\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)))\,=\,{\mathfrak{w}}_{p}(x)\quad\text{if ${\mathtt{l}}_{p}(x)<{\mathtt{r}}_{p}(x)$}\;.\end{gathered}

(2.27)

where

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{w}}_{p}(x)}\,:=\,\frac{\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{p}(x)}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}}{\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{p}(x)}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}}\;\;\text{if}\;\;x\not\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{p}(x)\\
\text{and}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{w}}_{p}(x)}\,:=\,\frac{1/2\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(x)}\,+\,\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{-}_{p}(x)}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}}{\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{p}(x)}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}}\;\;\text{if}\;\;x\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{p}(x)\;.\end{gathered}

(2.28)

We give in Remark
3.4
an interpretation for

{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,\cdot)
in terms of the diffusion on
{\mathbb{R}}
induced by
the generator
{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
.

For
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, denote by
p_{t}^{(p)}(\cdot,\,\cdot)
,
t>0
,
the transition probability of the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
. Let

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(0)}\equiv 1
and

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(\mathfrak{q}+1)}\equiv\infty

for convenience.

Theorem 2.5
.

Fix a continuous function
u_{0}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Denote by

u_{\epsilon}
the solution of the parabolic equation (
1.3
).

(a)

For all
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
t>0
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,u_{\epsilon}(\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}t\,,\,x)\ =\ \sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}p_{t}^{(p)}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell))\,\sum_{m^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell)}\frac{\pi(m^{\prime})}{\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell))}\,u_{0}(m^{\prime})\ .

(b)

For all
1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and sequence

(\varrho_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
such that

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}\,\prec\,\varrho_{\epsilon}\,\prec\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p+1)}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,u_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon}\,,\,x)\ =\ \sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\,{\mathtt{h}}_{p+1}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k))\sum_{m^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k)}\frac{\pi(m^{\prime})}{\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(k))}\,u_{0}(m^{\prime})\;.

(c)

Fix
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and a sequence

(\varrho_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
such that

\epsilon^{-1}\prec\varrho_{\epsilon}\prec\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}
. Denote by
m_{{\mathtt{l}}}(x)
,
m_{{\mathtt{r}}}(x)

the
S
-local minimum to the left, right of
x
, respectively, and by

\sigma
the
S
-local maximum between
m_{{\mathtt{l}}}(x)
and

m_{{\mathtt{r}}}(x)
, Then,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}u_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon}\,,\,x)\,=\,\left\{\begin{aligned} &u_{0}(m_{{\mathtt{l}}}(x))\;\;\text{if}\;\;x<\sigma\,,\\
&u_{0}(m_{{\mathtt{r}}}(x))\;\;\text{if}\;\;x>\sigma\,,\\
&(1/2)\,[\,u_{0}(m_{{\mathtt{l}}}(x))+u_{0}(m_{{\mathtt{r}}}(x))\,]\;\;\text{if}\;\;x=\sigma\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Remark 2.6
.

Assertion (a) for
p=1
is Theorem
2.1
. The claim for

1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
has to be understood as follows. Starting from
x
, the
set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
is reached with probability

{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
. From
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
it moves to

{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell)
at time
t
with probability

p_{t}^{(p)}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell))
. Being at
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell)
,
its position among the elements of this set is distributed according
to the probability measure
\pi(\cdot)/\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell))
.

Assertion (b) has a similar interpretation. In the time-scale

\varrho_{\epsilon}
, starting from
x
, the set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
is reached
with probability
{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
. Being at
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
,
its position among the states of this set is distributed according to
the probability measure
\pi(\cdot)/\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
.

It is not difficult to show that the limit, as
t\to\infty
, of item
(a) right-hand side for
1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
, converges to item (b)
right-hand side with
p+1
.

Remark 2.7
.

In part (c) of the theorem, the condition

\varrho_{\epsilon}\succ\epsilon^{-1}
is imposed to include the
point
x=\sigma
. If one assumes that
x\neq\sigma
, then it is
enough to require that
\varrho_{\epsilon}\succ 1
.

The last layer in the hierarchical construction.

By construction, either all states of the Markov chain

{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
are transient, or
{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is an
irreducibles class.

Claim 2.A:
Suppose that

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j\pm 1))=0
for some

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k\mp 1))>0
for all

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Suppose, without loss of generality, that

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j-1))=0
for some

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In this case,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j+1))>0
. Indeed, if
this rate vanishes, as
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
jumps only to nearest
neighbour sites,
{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j)
would be an absorbing state.
This contradicts the fact that
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
has no bounded
irreducibles class.

If
R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+1))=0
for some

k>j
, the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
is confined to the set

\{{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),\dots,{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k)\}
. It has therefore, a
closed irreducible class contained in this set. This contradicts once
more the fact that
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
has no bounded irreducibles
class. Hence,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+1))>0
for all

k>j
. By postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{9}({\mathfrak{q}})
,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k^{\prime}),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k^{\prime}+1))>0
for all

k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, as claimed.

Claim 2.B:
Suppose that
S(1)<S(0)
. Then,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j-1))=0
for some

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

By construction,
{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\geq 1
, and by postulate

{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathfrak{q}})
,

{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j+{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})={\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j)+1
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Then, by postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{4}({\mathfrak{q}})
, which states that
S(\cdot)
is
constant at each set
{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k)
,

S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}))\,<\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(0))\,.

(2.29)

It follows from this bound that
R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j-1))=0
for some
0<j\leq{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
. Indeed, if all
rates were positive, by postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{8}({\mathfrak{q}})
,
S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(0))\leq S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}))
, in contradiction with
(
2.29
).

Claim 2.C:
Suppose that
S(1)=S(0)
. Then,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j\pm 1))>0
for all

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Moreover, the measure
\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j))
satisfies
the detailed balance conditions.

By the argument leading to (
2.29
),

S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}))=S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(0))
.

Suppose, by contradiction, that

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j-1))=0
for some

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then, by Claim 2.A,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+1))>0
for all

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. In particular,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j-1),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j))>0
. Hence, by
postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{8}({\mathfrak{q}})
,

S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j))<S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j-1))
.

On the other hand, by postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{8}({\mathfrak{q}})
, since

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+1))>0
for all

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k))
is a non-increasing sequence. As

S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}))=S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(0))
, it is
constant. This contradicts the inequality
S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j))<S({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j-1))
obtained in the previous paragraph.

The measure
\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j))
satisfies the detailed balance
conditions in view of the definition (
2.22
) of the jump rates

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

Recall from postulates
{\mathcal{P}}_{3}({\mathfrak{q}})
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{9}({\mathfrak{q}})
that

{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j+{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})={\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j)+1
,

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and that the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
jump
rates are
{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
-periodic. It is therefore natural to
consider the evolution of
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
among the
equivalence classes.

Fix a positive integer
\ell\geq 1
, and let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}}:=\{{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}(j):0\leq j<\ell{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\}
. Denote by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}(\cdot)
the Markov chain

{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
projected on the sets
{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}
. Hence,
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}(\cdot)
is the

{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}
-valued Markov chain whose jump rates are given
by (
2.22
), where the sum is taken modulo
\ell\,{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

By Claim 2.C, if
S(0)=S(1)
, the probability measure

\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}
defined on

{\mathscr{S}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j))}\,:=\,\frac{1}{\ell}\,\frac{\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j))}{\sum_{0\leq i<{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(i))}\;,\quad 0\leq j<\ell\,{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\,.

(2.30)

is reversible for the Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}(\cdot)
.

If
S(0)\neq S(1)
, there is no explicit formula for the stationary
state, which exists and is unique because the chain

{\mathbb{X}}_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}(\cdot)
is irreducible. We denote the
stationary state by
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}
, as well.

Theorem 2.8
.

Fix a continuous function
u_{0}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
\ell
-periodic
for some
\ell\geq 1
, and a sequence

(\varrho_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
such that

\varrho_{\epsilon}\,\succ\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(\mathfrak{q})}
.
Denote by
u_{\epsilon}
the solution of the parabolic equation
(
1.3
). Then,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\,u_{\epsilon}(\varrho_{\epsilon},x)\,=\,\sum_{k=0}^{\ell{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}-1}\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}},\ell}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k)))\,\sum_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k)}\frac{\pi(m)}{\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k))}\,u_{0}(m)

for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

3.
Sketch of the proof

The proof of Theorems
2.1
and
2.5
is based on the
stochastic representation of the solutions of the parabolic equation
(
1.3
). In particular, all quantities appearing in the statements
of these results have a simple stochastic interpretation in terms of a
diffusion process.

Consider the one-dimensional diffusion process

dX_{\epsilon}(t)\;=\;{\textsf{b}}(X_{\epsilon}(t))\,dt\;+\;\sqrt{2\,\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}(X_{\epsilon}(t))}\,dW_{t}\;,

(3.1)

where
W_{t}
is the Brownian motion on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{T}}=[0,1)
be the one-dimensional torus of length
one, and let
\Pi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{T}}
be the projection given by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Pi(x)=x-\lfloor x\rfloor
,
\lfloor x\rfloor
being
the integer part of
x
.
If the integral of
{\textsf{b}}/{\textsf{a}}
on the interval
[0,1]
vanishes,

S(1)=0
, then the potential
S(\cdot)
is periodic, and one can
define a probability measure
\mu_{\epsilon}
on the torus
{\mathbb{T}}
by

\mu_{\epsilon}(d\theta)\,=\,\frac{1}{Z_{\epsilon}}\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(\theta)}\,e^{-S(\theta)/\epsilon}\,d\theta\;,

where
Z_{\epsilon}
is the normalization constant which turns

\mu_{\epsilon}
into a probability measure. A simple computation shows
that the projected diffusion

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{X}}_{\epsilon}(t)}:=\Pi(X_{\epsilon}(t))
is
reversible with respect to the measure
\mu_{\epsilon}
. In particular,

\mu_{\epsilon}
is a stationary state for the diffusion

{\mathtt{X}}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
. Note, however, that we did not assume in
the previous section that
S(\cdot)
is a periodic function.

Denote by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}N(x,r)
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
r>0
, the connected
component of the set
\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}:S(y)<S(x)+r\}
which contains

x
, and by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B(x,r)
the open ball of radius
r
centered
at
x
. Let Fix
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}0<r_{0}<{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
, and let
{\mathscr{E}}(m)
,

m\in{\mathcal{M}}
, be the well given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{E}}(m)}\,:=\,N(m,r_{0})\,\cap\,B(m,r_{0})\;.

(3.2)

For each
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
we say that
{\mathscr{E}}(m)
is a well. By definition
of
r_{0}
,
{\mathcal{E}}(m)\cap{\mathcal{E}}(m^{\prime})=\varnothing
if
m\neq m^{\prime}
. In
particular, by (
1.2
), the wells are ordered so that

{\mathcal{E}}(m_{j})<{\mathcal{E}}(m_{k})
for
j<k
. For
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, let

{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, be the wells defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}\,:=\,\bigcup_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}{\mathscr{E}}(m)\;,

(3.3)

Denote by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathscr{F}})
,
{\mathscr{F}}
a metric space,
the space of continuous trajectories with the locally uniform topology
and its Borel
\sigma
-algebra, and by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}D({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathscr{F}})
the space of right-continuous
trajectories with left limits endowed with the Skorohod topology and
its Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}
,

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, be the probability
measure on
C({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
induced by the diffusion

X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
,
X_{\epsilon}(\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,\cdot)

starting from
x
, respectively. Expectation with respect to

{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}
,
{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}
is represented by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{E}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}
,
respectively. Similarly, let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{Q}}^{p}_{\nu}
,

1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
\nu
a probability measure on
{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
, be the
probability measure on
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathscr{S}}_{p})
induced by the
Markov chain
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
starting from
\nu
. Expectation with
respect to
{\mathtt{Q}}^{p}_{\nu}
is represented by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{E}}^{p}_{\nu}
. When
\nu
is the Dirac measure
concentrated on a set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we denote

{\mathtt{Q}}^{p}_{\nu}
,
{\mathtt{E}}^{p}_{\nu}
by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{Q}}^{p}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}
,

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{E}}^{p}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}
, respectively.

The next theorem is the main result of this section. It describes the
asymptotic behavior of the diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
in terms of a
Markov chain on a countable state space. Recall the definition of the
probability measure
{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,\cdot)
,
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,

x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, on
{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
, introduced in (
2.27
).

Theorem 3.2

(Convergence of the finite-dimensional distributions)
.

For each
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\,\mathbb{P}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}\Big[\,\bigcap_{j=1}^{{\mathfrak{n}}}\big\{\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{j})\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{j}))\,\big\}\,\Big]\ =\ {\mathtt{Q}}^{(p)}_{{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,\cdot)}\Big[\,\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}\big\{\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{j})={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{j})\,\big\}\,\Big]

for all
{\mathfrak{n}}\geq 1
,
0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
,

k_{1},\dots,k_{{\mathfrak{n}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Theorems
2.1
and
2.5
are simple consequences of this
result. We present a proof at the end of this section. Following the
method introduced in
[
21
]
, we prove Theorem
3.2
, in two
steps. We first show in Theorem
3.3
that the solutions of the
resolvent equations are asymptotically constant on each well. Then,
following
[
20
]
, we deduce from this result the convergence of
the finite-dimensional distributions of
X_{\epsilon}
in the
time-scales
\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}
.

For
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
\lambda>0
, and a bounded function

g\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, denote by
G\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
the
lifted function defined by (
1.7
) with

{\mathfrak{g}}(k)=g({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Let
\phi_{p,\epsilon}

be the unique weak solution of the resolvent equation (
1.6
). We
refer to Appendix
B
for the definition of weak solutions,
and a proof of the existence and uniqueness.

Theorem 3.3
.

For every
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
\lambda>0
and bounded function

g\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}|\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)-f({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,|\,=\,0\,,

where
f\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the unique bounded solution of
the reduced resolvent equation

(\lambda-{\mathbb{L}}_{p})f=g\,.

(3.4)

Remark 3.4
.

Denote by
\tau(\mathscr{A})
,
\mathscr{A}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
,
the hitting time of the set
\mathscr{A}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tau({\mathscr{A}})}\ :=\ \inf\big\{\,t>0\,:\,X_{\epsilon}(t)\in{\mathscr{A}}\,\big\}\;.

(3.5)

By Corollary
4.9
,

{\mathtt{h}}_{p}(x,{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))\,=\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}\big[\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))\,=\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})\big]\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)

for all
2\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Here and below,

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}o_{\epsilon}(1)
stands for an expression which converges
to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, uniformly in the parameters on which it
depends.

4.
Hitting times

In this section, we present general estimates on the hitting times of
the diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
. Denote by
{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot)
an
element of
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
, and by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{P}}^{\vartheta,\epsilon}_{x}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the
probability measure on
{\mathscr{D}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
induced by the
diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
starting from
x
and speeded-up by

\vartheta
. Expectation with respect to

{\mathbb{P}}^{\vartheta,\epsilon}_{x}
is represented by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{E}}^{\vartheta,\epsilon}_{x}
.

For every closed set
A\subset{\mathbb{T}}
let

\tau(A)
be the first time the trajectory
{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot)
hits the
set
A
, that is

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tau(A)}\,:=\,\inf\{t\geq 0:{\mathtt{x}}(t)\in A\}.

(4.1)

If
A
is a singleton or a pair, say
A=\{a\}
,
A=\{a,b\}
for
some
a
,
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we write
\tau(a)
,
\tau(a,b)
for

\tau(\{a\})
,
\tau(\{a,b\})
, respectively.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
F\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a bounded and measurable
function. Fix
\lambda>0
,
\vartheta>0
, and denote by

\psi=\psi_{\epsilon,\vartheta,\lambda,F}
the solution of the
resolvent equation

\{\lambda-\vartheta\,{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}\}\,\psi=F\,.

Then,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\|\psi\|_{\infty}}\,:=\,\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|\psi(x)|\,\leq\,\frac{1}{\lambda}\,\|F\|_{\infty}\;.

(4.2)

Moreover,

\Big|\,\psi(x)-\psi(a)\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\,\tau(a)<\tau(b)\,]-\psi(b)\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\,\tau(b)<\tau(a)\,]\,\Big|\,\leq\,2\,\|F\|_{\infty}\;{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\,\tau(a,b)/\vartheta\,]\;,

and

\big|\,\psi(x)-\psi(a)\,\big|\,\leq\,2\,\|F\|_{\infty}\;\Big\{\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\,\tau(a,b)/\vartheta\,]\;+\;\frac{1}{\lambda}\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\,\tau(b)<\tau(a)\,]\,\Big\}

(4.3)

for all
a<x<b
.

Proof.

Recall from
[
12
, Section 6.5]
the stochastic representation of
the solution of the resolvent equation:

\psi(x)={\mathbb{E}}^{\vartheta,\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}\,F({\mathtt{x}}(s))\;ds\,\Big]\;.

(4.4)

Assertion (
4.2
) is a simple consequence of this formula.

Fix
a<x<b
and let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\tau:=\tau(a,b)
. By the
stochastic representation (
4.4
) of the resolvent equation, and
changing the measure from
{\mathbb{P}}^{\vartheta,\epsilon}_{x}
to

{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}
,

\displaystyle\psi(x)

\displaystyle=\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda s}\,F({\mathtt{x}}(s\vartheta))\;ds\,\Big]

\displaystyle=\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,\int_{0}^{\tau/\vartheta}e^{-\lambda s}\,F({\mathtt{x}}(s\vartheta))\,ds\,\Big]\,+\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,\int_{\tau/\vartheta}^{\infty}\,e^{-\lambda s}\,F({\mathtt{x}}(s\vartheta))\,ds\,\Big]\,.

The absolute value of the first term is bounded by

\|F\|_{\infty}\;{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\,\tau/\vartheta\,]
. After a change of variables, by the strong Markov property and
the stochastic representation of the solution of the resolvent
equation, the second term is seen to be equal to

{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,e^{-\lambda\tau/\vartheta}\,\psi({\mathtt{x}}(\tau))\,\big]\;.

Decomposing this expectation according to the events

\{\tau(a)<\tau(b)\}
and

\{\tau(b)<\tau(a)\}
the previous expectation
can be written as

\psi(a)\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,e^{-\lambda\tau/\vartheta}\,,\,\tau(a)<\tau(b)\,\big]\,+\,\psi(b)\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,e^{-\lambda\tau/\vartheta}\,,\,\tau(b)<\tau(a)\,\big]\;.

Add and subtract
1
to
\exp\{-\lambda\tau/\vartheta\}
to
rewrite this expression as

\psi(a)\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(a)<\tau(b)\,\big]\,+\,\psi(b)\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(b)<\tau(a)\,\big]\;+\;R(\epsilon)\;,

(4.5)

where

\big|R(\epsilon)\big|\,\leq\,\max\big\{|\psi(a)|\,,\,|\psi(b)|\big\}\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,1-e^{-\lambda\tau/\vartheta}\,\Big]\;.

As the absolute value of
\psi(\cdot)
is uniformly bounded
by
(1/\lambda)\,\|F\|_{\infty}
, and
1-e^{-x}\leq x
for

x\geq 0
,

\big|R(\epsilon)\big|\,\leq\,\|F\|_{\infty}\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau/\vartheta\,\big]\;.

This proves the second assertion of the lemma.

To prove the last assertion of the lemma, return to (
4.5
), and
write the sum of the first two terms as

\psi(a)\,\,+\,\big[\,\psi(b)\,-\,\psi(a)\big]\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(b)<\tau(a)\,\big]\;.

To complete the proof it remains to recall the previous estimates and
that the absolute value of
\psi(\cdot)
is uniformly bounded
by
(1/\lambda)\,\|F\|_{\infty}
.
∎

The next result provides bounds on the expectation of the hitting time

\tau(a,b)
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Given
a<b
, let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}K=K_{a,b}=K^{{}^{\nearrow}}_{a,b}\wedge K^{{}^{\nwarrow}}_{a,b}
, where

K^{{}^{\nearrow}}_{a,b}\,:=\,\sup_{a\leq y\leq x\leq b}\{\,S(x)-S(y)\,\}\,,\quad K^{{}^{\nwarrow}}_{a,b}\,:=\,\sup_{a\leq y\leq x\leq b}\{\,S(y)-S(x)\,\}\;.

Then,

\sup_{x\;\in\;[a,b]}{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(a,b)\,\big]\,\leq\,{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}\,\epsilon^{-1}\,e^{K_{a,b}/\epsilon}

for all
\epsilon>0
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}}:=\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\textsf{a}}(x)^{-1}
.

Proof.

Consider the equation

\begin{cases}({\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}u)(x)=1\;\;\text{if}\ x\in(a,b)\\
u(a)=u^{\prime}(a)=0\,.\end{cases}

(4.6)

By a direct computation it can be verified that the function

\zeta_{\epsilon}:(a,b)\to{\mathbb{R}}
given by

\zeta_{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{a}^{x}e^{\frac{S(y)-S(a)}{\epsilon}}\int_{a}^{y}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(z)}\,e^{\frac{S(a)-S(z)}{\epsilon}}dzdy

(4.7)

solves the previous equation. Note that
\zeta_{\epsilon}
is of class

C^{2}
on
(a,b)
and positive. Let
\xi_{\epsilon}
be an extension
of
\zeta_{\epsilon}
to an element in
C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. In other words,

\xi_{\epsilon}\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
, has compact support, and satisfies

\xi_{\epsilon}(x)=\zeta_{\epsilon}(x)
for every
x\in(a,b)
.

Fix
x_{0}\in[a,b]
. Since
\xi_{\epsilon}
is in the domain of
the generator
{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
, the following expression is a
martingale:

M_{t}\,=\,\xi_{\epsilon}(X_{\epsilon}(t))-\int_{0}^{t}{\mathscr{[}}{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}\xi_{\epsilon}](X_{\epsilon}(s))\;ds\,.

Let
\tau:=\tau(a,b)
. By (
4.6
) and the definition of

\xi_{\epsilon}

M_{t\wedge\tau}\,=\,\zeta_{\epsilon}(X_{\epsilon}(t\wedge\tau))\,-\,t\wedge\tau,\quad\forall t\geq 0.

Therefore,

\zeta_{\epsilon}(x_{0})\;=\;{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x_{0}}\big[M_{0}\big]\;=\;{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x_{0}}\big[M_{t\wedge\tau}\big]\;=\;{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x_{0}}\big[\zeta_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{x}}(t\wedge\tau))\big]\;-\;{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x_{0}}\big[t\wedge\tau\big]\,,

so that

{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x_{0}}\big[\,\tau\,\big]\,=\,\lim_{t\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x_{0}}\big[\,t\wedge\tau\,\big]\;=\;{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x_{0}}\big[\,\zeta_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{x}}(\tau))\,\big]\,-\,\zeta_{\epsilon}(x_{0})\;.

(4.8)

In particular, by the explicit expression of the positive function

\zeta_{\epsilon}
, and a direct computation,

{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x_{0}}[\tau]\,\leq\,\sup_{x\;\in\;[a,b]}\zeta_{\epsilon}(x)\;\leq\;{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}\,\epsilon^{-1}\,e^{K^{{}^{\nearrow}}_{a,b}/\epsilon}

for all
\epsilon>0
,
x_{0}\in[a,b]
, where, recall,

{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}=\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}[1/{\textsf{a}}(x)]
, and

K^{{}^{\nearrow}}_{a,b}=\sup_{a\leq y\leq x\leq b}\{\,S(x)-S(y)\,\}
.

By choosing
\zeta_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
as the solution of

\begin{cases}({\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}u)(x)=1\;\;\text{if}\ x\in(a,b)\\
u(b)=u^{\prime}(b)=0\,,\end{cases}

the same argument yields the alternative bound

\sup_{x\;\in\;[a,b]}{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(a,b)\,\big]\,\leq\,{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}\,\epsilon^{-1}\,e^{K^{{}^{\nwarrow}}_{a,b}/\epsilon}

for all
\epsilon>0
, where

K^{{}^{\nwarrow}}_{a,b}\,:=\,\sup_{a\leq y\leq x\leq b}\{\,S(y)-S(x)\,\}
. This
completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The next result shows that the set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
is attained in the
time-scale
\epsilon^{-1}e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}/\epsilon}
. It will be
improved in Corollary
7.4
, after we prove the metastable
behavior of the diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
at time-scale

\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

There exists finite constant
C_{0}=C_{0}({\mathfrak{q}},{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot))
such
that

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})\,]\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\epsilon^{-1}\,e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}/\epsilon}

for all
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, provided
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=0
.

Proof.

The proof is carried by induction on
p
. By Lemma
4.2
, as

{\mathscr{M}}_{1}={\mathcal{M}}
contains all
S
-local minima,

{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{1})\,]\leq{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}\,\epsilon^{-1}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

We turn to
p\geq 2
. Bound
\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})
by

\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1})+\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})\circ\vartheta_{\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1})}
, where
(\vartheta_{r}:r\geq 0)
represents the group of
translations of a trajectory. By the strong Markov property,

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})\,]\,\leq\,\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1})\,]\,+\,\sup_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}}{\mathbb{E}}_{m}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})\,]\,.

Fix
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}
. If
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
,
\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})=0
, and there is nothing to prove.

Assume, therefore that
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(j)
for some
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
and that
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(j)\cap{\mathscr{M}}_{p}=\varnothing
. This means that

{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(j)
is a
{\mathbb{X}}_{p-1}
-transient state. By periodicity of
the jump rates,
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(j)
may reach a
{\mathbb{X}}_{p-1}
-recurrent
state in a finite number of jumps. Therefore, by Proposition

A.2
, Lemma
A.11
, and postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{7}(p-1)
, if

{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(j)
can reach a set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
with
jumps to the right (
j<\ell
),

K^{{}^{\nearrow}}_{m,m^{-}_{p-1,\ell}}\leq{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}
. Thus, by Lemma

4.2
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{m}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})\,]\,\leq\,{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}\,\epsilon^{-1}\,e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}/\epsilon}\;.

A similar bound can be obtained if
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(j)
can reach the set

{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(\ell)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
with jumps to the left. To complete
the proof, it remains to recollect all previous estimates.
∎

The next result is the main estimate needed in the proof of Corollary

4.7
which states that the diffusion
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
may not
escape from a well quickly.

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix
b_{l}<b_{r}
such that
S(x)<S(b_{l})=S(b_{r})
for all

b_{l}<x<b_{r}
. Let
a\in(b_{l},b_{r})
such that

S(a)=\min\{S(x):x\in[b_{l},b_{r}]\}
. Then, for all
t>0
,

\delta>0
, there exist a finite contant
C_{0}
, depending only on

{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot)
, and
\eta=\eta(\delta)
,

0<\eta<\min\{b_{r}-a,a-b_{l}\}/2
such that

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau(b_{l},b_{r})\leq t\,\big]\,\leq\,\frac{C_{0}}{\eta^{2}}\,\big\{\,\epsilon\,t+(x-a)^{2}e^{K(x)/\epsilon}\,\big\}\,e^{-[S(b_{r})-S(a)-\delta]/\epsilon}

for all
x\in(b_{l},b_{r})
. Here,

K(x)\,=\,K^{{}^{\nearrow}}_{a,x}\;\;\text{if $x>a$ and}\;\;K(x)\,=\,K^{{}^{\nwarrow}}_{x,a}\;\;\text{if $x<a$}\;.

Proof.

Denote by
\zeta_{\epsilon}
the solution of the equation (
4.6
) in
the interval
(b_{l},b_{r})
. Let
\tau=\tau(b_{l},b_{r})
. By the
displayed equation above (
4.8
),

{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\zeta_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{x}}(t\wedge\tau))\,\big]\,=\,\zeta_{\epsilon}(x)\,+\,{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,t\wedge\tau\,\big]

(4.9)

for all
x\in(b_{l},b_{r})
,
t>0
.

We estimate separately each term of the previous identity. Assume,
without loss of generality, that
a\leq x\leq b_{r}
. The second
term on the right-hand site is bounded by
t
. The first one, by the
explicit formula (
4.7
) for
\zeta_{\epsilon}
, is bounded by

\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}}{\epsilon}\,(x-a)^{2}\,e^{\sup_{a\leq z\leq y\leq x}[S(y)-S(z)]/\epsilon}\,=\,\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}}{\epsilon}\,(x-a)^{2}\,e^{K^{{}^{\nearrow}}_{a,x}/\epsilon}

where
{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}
,
K^{{}^{\nearrow}}_{a,x}
have been introduced in the
statement of Lemma
4.2
. .

We turn to the term on the left-hand side of (
4.9
). Since

\zeta_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
is positive, it is bounded below by

{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\zeta_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{x}}(\tau))\,{\mathtt{1}}\{\tau\leq t\}\big]\,\geq\,\min_{x^{\prime}\in\{b_{l},b_{r}\}}\zeta_{\epsilon}(x^{\prime})\,\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau\leq t\big]\,.

We estimate
\zeta_{\epsilon}(b_{r})
, the same argument applies to

b_{l}
. Fix
\delta>0
. There exists
0<\eta<(b_{r}-a)/2
such that

|S(y)-S(b_{r})|\leq\delta/2
for all
|y-b_{r}|\leq\eta
,

|S(z)-S(a)|\leq\delta/2
for all
|z-a|\leq\eta
.
By the explicit formula (
4.7
) for
\zeta_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
,

\zeta_{\epsilon}(b_{r})\,\geq\,\frac{c_{0}}{\epsilon}\,\int_{b_{r}-\eta}^{b_{r}}dy\int_{a}^{a+\eta}e^{[S(y)-S(z)]/\epsilon}\,dz\,\geq\,\frac{c_{0}}{\epsilon}\,\eta^{2}\,e^{[S(b_{r})-S(a)-\delta]/\epsilon}

for some positive constant
c_{0}
depending only on
{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot)
.
To complete the proof, it remains to recollect all previous
estimates.
∎

Fix
2\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
. Denote
by
m_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}}
,
m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}
the first element to left,
right of
m
which belongs to
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
,
respectively:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}}\,:=\,\min\big\{m^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}_{p}:m^{\prime}>m\big\}\,,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}}\,:=\,\max\big\{m^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}\setminus{\mathscr{M}}_{p}:m^{\prime}<m\big\}\;.

Lemma 4.5
.

Fix
2\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
. There exists
\kappa>0
such that

\Lambda(m,m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})\,-\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,>\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}\,+\,\kappa\;,\quad\Lambda(m,m_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}})\,-\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,>\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}\,+\,\kappa

(4.10)

for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

We prove the assertion for
m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}
, the same argument apply
to
m_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}}
. By definition,

m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell)
for some
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. On the
other hand, as
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
, and
m<m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}
,

m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(j)
for some
j\leq\ell
. Note that
j
cannot be equal
to
\ell
because
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
and

m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}\not\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
. Moreover, by definition of

m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}
,
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(i)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
for all

j\leq i<\ell
. Recall that
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
.

Case A
: Suppose that

{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k^{\prime})
for some
k^{\prime}\neq k
.

In this case, as the sets
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(i)
are ordered,
k<k^{\prime}
. By
postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{6}(p)
,

\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1))-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\geq{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}
.
Since
k^{\prime}\geq k+1
and
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k^{\prime})
,

\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1))\leq\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k^{\prime}))\leq\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1))
. As

\ell-1<\ell
and
m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell)
,

\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1))\leq\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})
. Finally, as
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
,
\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})\leq\Lambda(m,m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})
. Collecting all previous estimates yields that

\Lambda(m,m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})\,-\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\geq\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}\;,

as claimed since
{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}
.

Case B
: Suppose that

{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
.

As
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
,

{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell)\cap{\mathscr{M}}_{p}=\varnothing
,
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1)

is
{\mathbb{X}}_{p-1}
-recurrent and
{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell)
is
{\mathbb{X}}_{p-1}

transient. Hence,

R_{p-1}({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1),{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell))=0
. Thus, by
postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{6}(p-1)
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{7}(p-1)
,

\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1),{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell))\,-\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1))\,>\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}\;.

As
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(j)
,
j\leq\ell-1
, and
m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell)
,
\Lambda({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1),{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell))\leq\Lambda(m,m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})
, so that

\Lambda(m,m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})\,-\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1))\,>\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}\;.

Since
