{\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1)
and
\{m\}
are contained in

{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
, by postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{4}(p)
,

S({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}(\ell-1))=S(m)=S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
. Since

{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\mathtt{b}}(\cdot)
are periodic,

\Lambda(m^{+}_{p,k},m_{r})\,-\,S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
takes only a finite
number of distinct values. Therefore, in the previous inequality we
may substitute
{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}
by
{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa
for some

\kappa>0
, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 4.6
.

Fix
2\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
. There exists
\kappa>0
satisfying the
following property. For all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
, there
exist
m_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}}<\sigma_{l}<m<\sigma_{r}<m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}

such that

\begin{gathered}S(m)\,\leq\,S(x)\,<\,S(\sigma_{l})\,=\,S(\sigma_{r})\;\;\text{for all}\;\;x\in(\sigma_{l},\sigma_{r})\;,\\
\text{and}\;\;S(\sigma_{r})\,>\,S(m)\,+\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}\,+\,\kappa\;.\end{gathered}

(4.11)

Proof.

To prove this assertion, let
\sigma_{r}
,
\sigma_{l}
be the first

S
-local maxima on the right, left of
m
which attain

\Lambda(m_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}},m)
,
\Lambda(m,m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})
,
respectively:

\displaystyle\sigma_{r}\,=\,\min\big\{\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}:\sigma^{\prime}>m\,,\,S(\sigma^{\prime})=\Lambda(m,m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}})\,\big\}\,,

\displaystyle\sigma_{l}\,=\,\max\big\{\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{W}}:\sigma^{\prime}<m\,,\,S(\sigma^{\prime})=\Lambda(m_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}},m)\,\big\}\;.

By definition,

m_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}}<\sigma_{l}<m<\sigma_{r}<m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}
. By
Lemma
4.5
, the second line of (
4.11
) holds for some

\kappa>0
.

Recall from (
A.5
) the definition of
\sigma_{j,j+1}^{p,\pm}
,

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. If
\sigma_{r}>\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,-}
, replace
\sigma_{r}

by
\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,-}
. By postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{6}(p)
and the
definition of
\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,-}
,

S(\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,-})-S(m)=S(\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,-})-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\geq{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}>{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}
. Thus, if

\sigma_{r}>\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,-}
and we perform the replacement, the
second line of (
4.11
) still holds for some
\kappa>0
, and

\sigma_{r}\leq\sigma_{k,k+1}^{p,-}
. A similar procedure has to be
carried out for
\sigma_{l}
.

Since

\sigma_{k-1,k}^{+}\leq\sigma_{l}<m<\sigma_{r}\leq\sigma_{k,k+1}^{-}
,
and
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
, by Proposition
A.2
, and the previous
bounds,

S(m)\,\leq\,S(x)\;\;\text{for all}\;\;x\in(\sigma_{l},\sigma_{r})\,,\;\;\text{and}\;\;S(\sigma_{l})\wedge S(\sigma_{r})\,>\,S(m)\,+\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}\,+\,\kappa

(4.12)

for some
\kappa>0
. If
S(x)\geq S(\sigma_{l})\wedge S(\sigma_{r})
for
some
m\leq x<\sigma_{r}
, replace
\sigma_{r}
by the first point

\sigma^{\prime}
to the right of
m
such that

S(\sigma^{\prime})=S(\sigma_{l})\wedge S(\sigma_{r})
. Do the same thing for

\sigma_{l}
. Clearly (
4.12
) still holds. Moreover,

S(\sigma_{l})=S(\sigma_{r})
and
S(x)<S(\sigma_{l})
for all

x\in(\sigma_{l},\sigma_{r})
. The constant
\kappa
can be taken
independent of
k
due to the periodicity of
{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot)
,

{\mathtt{b}}(\cdot)
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We have now all elements to show that the diffusion cannot leave a
well in a short time. Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}:=\cup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
, where

{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
has been introduced in (
3.3
).

Corollary 4.7
.

Fix
2\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
. Then, there exist
\kappa_{0}>0
and a finite

C_{0}=C_{0}({\mathtt{a}}(\cdot),\kappa_{0})
, such that

\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sup_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}{\mathbb{P}}_{m}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{E}}_{p-1}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}_{p})\,\leq\,e^{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa_{0}]/\epsilon}\,\big]\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\epsilon\,e^{-\kappa_{0}/\epsilon}\;.

Proof.

Fix
2\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
. Let

\kappa>0
,
\sigma_{l}
,
\sigma_{r}\in{\mathbb{R}}
be the elements given by
Lemma
4.6
and set
\kappa_{0}=\kappa/3
. As

m_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}}<\sigma_{l}<m<\sigma_{r}<m_{p-1,{\mathtt{r}}}
,
starting from
m
,
\tau(\sigma_{l},\sigma_{r})\leq\tau({\mathscr{E}}_{p-1}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}_{p})
. Hence,

{\mathbb{P}}_{m}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{E}}_{p-1}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}_{p})\,\leq\,e^{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa_{0}]/\epsilon}\,\big]\,\leq\,{\mathbb{P}}_{m}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau(\sigma_{l},\sigma_{r})\leq e^{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa_{0}]/\epsilon}\,\big]\;.

By Lemma
4.6
, the hypotheses of Lemma
4.4
are fulfilled,
and
S(m)=\min\{S(x):x\in[\sigma_{l},\sigma_{r}]\}
. Therefore, by
Lemma
4.4
with
a=x=m
,
\delta=\kappa_{0}
,

t=e^{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa_{0}]/\epsilon}

{\mathbb{P}}_{m}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau(\sigma_{l},\sigma_{r})\leq e^{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa_{0}]/\epsilon}\,\big]\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\epsilon\,e^{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa_{0}]/\epsilon}\,e^{-[S(\sigma_{r})-S(m)-\kappa_{0}]/\epsilon}

for some finite constant
C_{0}=C_{0}({\mathtt{a}}(\cdot),\kappa_{0})
, and whose
value may change from line to line. By Lemma
4.6
,
S(\sigma_{r})-S(m)\geq{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa={\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+3\kappa_{0}
. in view of
the previous estimates,

{\mathbb{P}}_{m}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{E}}_{p-1})\,\leq\,e^{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa_{0}]/\epsilon}\,\big]\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\epsilon\,e^{-\kappa_{0}/\epsilon}\;.

As
{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\mathtt{b}}(\cdot)
are periodic, this estimate is
uniform over
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

The next results provide bounds on the probability of the event

\{\tau(a)<\tau(b)\}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Fix
a<b
.
Then,

\begin{gathered}{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\tau(b)\;<\;\tau(a)\big]\,\leq\,\frac{(x-a)}{\eta}\,\exp\Big\{\,\big[\,\sup_{z\in[a,x]}S(z)-\inf_{y\in[b-\eta,b]}S(y)\big]/\epsilon\,\Big\}\;,\\
{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\tau(a)\;<\;\tau(b)\big]\,\leq\,\frac{(b-x)}{\eta}\,\exp\Big\{\,\big[\,\sup_{z\in[x,b]}S(z)-\inf_{y\in[a,a+\eta]}S(y)\big]/\epsilon\,\Big\}\end{gathered}

for all
\epsilon>0
,
a<x<b
,
0<\eta<b-a
.

Proof.

Consider the
equation

\begin{cases}({\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}u)(x)=0&\text{if}\ x\in(a,b)\\
u(a)=0,u^{\prime}(a)=1\end{cases}

(4.13)

and let
\zeta_{\epsilon}
be its unique solution. By a direct
computation, it can be verified that

\zeta_{\epsilon}(x)=\int_{a}^{x}e^{\frac{S(y)-S(a)}{\epsilon}}dy.

(4.14)

Fix
x\in(a,b)
,
0<\eta<b-a
. Let
\tau:=\tau(a,b)
. By
(
4.13
),

Y_{t}:=\zeta_{\epsilon}(X_{\epsilon}(t\wedge\tau))
is a well
defined bounded martingale. By the optional stopping theorem and
(
4.14
),

{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\tau(b)<\tau(a)\big]\,=\,\frac{\zeta_{\epsilon}(x)}{\zeta_{\epsilon}(b)}\,\leq\,\int_{a}^{x}e^{S(y)/\epsilon}\,dy\Big/\int_{b-\eta}^{b}e^{S(z)/\epsilon}\,dz\;.

(4.15)

To complete the proof of the first bound, it remains to estimate in
the last ratio
S(y)
by
\sup_{y^{\prime}\in[a,x]}S(y^{\prime})
and
S(z)
by

\inf_{z^{\prime}\in[b-\eta,b]}S(z^{\prime})
.

To obtain the second estimate, replace the boundary conditions in
(
4.13
) by
u(b)=0
,
u^{\prime}(b)=-1
, note that the solution is
given by

\zeta_{\epsilon}(x)\,=\,\int_{x}^{b}e^{\frac{S(y)-S(b)}{\epsilon}}\,dy\,,

and repeat the previous arguments.
∎

Corollary 4.9
.

Fix
a<b
such that
\max\{S(a),S(b)\}<\max_{x\in[a,b]}S(x)
. Denote by
{\mathfrak{S}}
the finite set where
S(\cdot)
attains its
global maximum in the interval
[a,b]
:

{\mathfrak{S}}\,=\,\big\{\sigma\in(a,b)\cap{\mathcal{W}}:S(\sigma)=\max_{x\in[a,b]}S(x)\big\}\;,

and enumerate
{\mathfrak{S}}
as
{\mathfrak{S}}=\{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{\ell}\}
. Fix
x\not\in{\mathfrak{S}}
. Then,

{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\tau(b)<\tau(a)\big]\,=\,\frac{\sum_{i\in I_{x}}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{i})}}{\sum_{1\leq j\leq\ell}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{j})}}\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;,

where
I_{x}=\{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}:1\leq i\leq\ell\,,\,\sigma_{i}<x\}
.

Proof.

The first identity in equation (
4.15
) provides an explicit formula
for
{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\tau(b)<\tau(a)]
. multiply the numerator
and the denominator by

(2\pi\epsilon)^{-1/2}e^{-S(\sigma_{1})/\epsilon}
. It remains to use
second order Taylor expansion to conclude.
∎

If
x\in{\mathfrak{S}}
, the same argument shows that

{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\tau(b)<\tau(a)\big]\,=\,\frac{1}{\sum_{1\leq j\leq\ell}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{j})}}\Big\{\sum_{i\in I_{x}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{i})}}\,+\,\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(x)}}\,\Big\}\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

(4.16)

5.
Proof of Theorem
3.3
for
p=1

The proof is divided in two steps. We first show that the solution of
the resolvent equation (
1.6
) is asymptotically constant on each
well and then that all limit points are solution of the reduced
resolvent equation (
3.4
). More precisely, in Proposition

5.2
, based on local ergodic properties of the diffusion, we show
that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\max_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sup_{x,y\in{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})}|\phi_{1,\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{1,\epsilon}(y)|=0\,.

(5.1)

Then, in Lemma
5.3
, we prove that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\max_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|\phi_{1,\epsilon}(m_{k})-f({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))|=0\,,

(5.2)

where
f(\cdot)
is the solution of the reduced resolvent equation
(
3.4
). Theorem
3.3
for
p=1
is an easy consequence of
these two results.

Local ergodicity

We start with the local ergodicity. We show that the solution of the
resolvent equation is asymptotically constant on each well by
guaranteeing that, for any
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
x\in{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})
, the
process
X_{\epsilon}(t)
, starting at
x
, hits the critical point

m_{k}
, before leaving the well
{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})
, in a time-scale much
smaller than the metastable scale
\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}
.

Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta_{\epsilon}(k)}\,:=\,\sqrt{\frac{2\,\epsilon\log\epsilon^{-1}}{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})}}\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\eta_{\epsilon}(k)}\,:=\,\frac{\epsilon}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)}\;=\;\sqrt{\frac{-\,\epsilon\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})}{2\,\log\epsilon^{-1}}}\;,\quad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;.

(5.3)

Denote by
J_{k}
,
J^{+}_{k}
,
J^{-}_{k}
the intervals given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J^{+}_{k}}\,:=\,[\,m_{k},\sigma_{k+1}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)\,]\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J^{-}_{k}}\,:=\,[\,\sigma_{k}+\delta_{\epsilon}(k),m_{k}\,]\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J_{k}}\,:=\,J^{-}_{k}\cup J^{+}_{k}\;.

The next result establishes the local ergodicity. Recall the
definition of
{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}
introduced in Lemma
4.2
.

Remark 5.1
.

The result below is much stronger than what is required to prove the
local ergodicity stated in (
5.1
) since it establishes that, for
each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\phi_{1,\epsilon}
is asymptotically constant in a
slightly smaller interval than the one formed by the two local maxima
containing the local minimum
m_{k}
. (In particular, for
\epsilon

small,
\phi_{1,\epsilon}
is almost a step function). This stronger
result is needed, however, in the proof of the convergence of

\phi_{1,\epsilon}
. More precisely, in the proof of Lemma

5.7
. See below equation (
5.15
).

Proposition 5.2
.

For each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\sup_{x\in J_{k}}|\phi_{1,\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{1,\epsilon}(m_{k})|\,\leq\,2\,\|g\|_{\infty}\,\Big\{\,\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}}{\epsilon\,\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}}\,+\,\frac{1}{\lambda}\,\frac{2\,\sqrt{2}\,\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)\wedge\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)}\,\Big\}

for all
\epsilon
sufficiently small. In particular, as

{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
are periodic, (
5.1
) holds.

Proof.

Fix
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and assume that
x\in J^{+}_{k}
. The same argument
applies to
x\in J^{-}_{k}
. The proof relies on Proposition
4.1

with
a=m_{k}
,
b=\sigma_{k+1}
,
F=G
and
\vartheta=\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}
. We need to estimate the two terms on the
right-hand side of (
4.3
).

On the one hand, since
S
is monotone on
J^{+}_{k}
, by Lemma

4.2
,

\sup_{x\in J^{+}_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(m_{k},\sigma_{k+1})\,\big]\,\leq\,\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}}{\epsilon}

for all
\epsilon>0
.

On the other hand, since

\sup_{x\in[m_{k},\sigma_{k+1}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)]}S(x)\,=\,S(\sigma_{k+1}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1))\;,\quad\inf_{x\in[\sigma_{k+1}-\eta,,\sigma_{k+1}]}S(x)\,=\,S(\sigma_{k+1}-\eta)\;,

for all
\eta<\sigma_{k+1}-m_{k}
, as
\sigma_{k+1}-m_{k}<1
, by
Lemma
4.8
with

\eta=(1/\sqrt{2})\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)
,

\sup_{x\in J^{+}_{k}}{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(\sigma_{k+1})\;<\;\tau(m_{k})\,\big]\,\leq\,\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)}\,e^{-r/\epsilon}\;,

where

r=S(\sigma_{k+1}-\eta)-S(\sigma_{k+1}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1))
. By a Taylor expansion and the definition
(
5.3
) of
\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)
,

r=(1/2)(1+o(\epsilon))\,\epsilon\,\log\epsilon^{-1}
. In
this formula and below,
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}o(\epsilon)
represents an
expression which depends only on
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,
{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
and
converges to
0
as
\epsilon\to 0
. Thus,

\sup_{x\in J^{+}_{k}}{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{\epsilon}\big[\,\tau(\sigma_{k+1})\;<\;\tau(m_{k})\,\big]\,\leq\,\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)}\,\sqrt{\epsilon}

for all
\epsilon
sufficiently small. To complete the proof of the
assertion of the lemma for
x\in J^{+}_{k}
, it remains to recall the
statement of Proposition
4.1
. The same argument applies to

J^{-}_{k}
.
∎

Convergence to
f

In this subsection, we prove (
5.2
).
Let
f_{\epsilon}\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))}\,:=\,\phi_{\epsilon}(m_{k})\;.

(5.4)

We wish to prove that
f_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
converges to
f(\cdot)

pointwisely. By Proposition
4.1
,
f_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
is
uniformly bounded by
(1/\lambda)\,\|g\|_{\infty}
. Thus, since,
by Lemma
5.8
, the reduced resolvent equation (
3.4
) has a
unique bounded solution, it is enough to show that all limit points of
the sequence
f_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
solve the resolvent equation.

Proposition 5.3
.

For all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}|\,f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))-f({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,|\,=\,0\,,

where
f(\cdot)
is the unique bounded solution of the reduced
resolvent equation (
3.4
).

To prove the convergence of the sequence
f_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
, we
construct a test function
Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
with support contained
in an interval
(a,b)
. As
\phi_{1,\epsilon}
is a weak solution of the
resolvent equation (
1.6
),

\displaystyle\lambda\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)\,\phi_{1,\epsilon}(x)\,e^{-S(x)/\epsilon}\,dx\,+\,\epsilon\,\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}[\,\partial_{x}\,({\textsf{a}}\,Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k})\,](x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{1,\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-S(x)/\epsilon}\,dx

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)\,G(x)\,e^{-S(x)/\epsilon}\,dx\;.

Taking the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
in this identity, we show that any
limit point of the sequence
f_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
solves the reduced
resolvent equation (
3.4
).

A test function
. Let

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}h_{k,\epsilon}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, be
the equilibrium potential defined as the solution of the Poisson
equation

\begin{cases}{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}u=0,&\text{if }x\not\in{\mathscr{E}}\\
u(x)=1&\text{if }x\in{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})\,,\\
u(x)=0&\text{if }x\in{\mathscr{E}}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})\;,\end{cases}\quad\text{where}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{E}}}\,:=\,\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})\;.

Clearly the equilibrium potential
h_{k,\epsilon}
has a stochastic
representation as

h_{k,\epsilon}(x)={\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\tau_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{E}}(m_{k}))<\tau_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{E}}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k}))\,]
. The test function

Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
is an approximation of
h_{k,\epsilon}(\cdot)
. Its precise definition requires some notation.

Recall from (
5.6
) the definition of the length

\eta_{k}(\epsilon)
. Denote by
C_{\epsilon}(k)
,

\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)
,
A_{\epsilon}(k)
the
core
sets given
by

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{\epsilon}(k)}\,:=\,[\sigma_{k}+\delta_{\epsilon}(k)+\eta_{\epsilon}(k)\,,\,\sigma_{k+1}-\eta_{\epsilon}(k+1)-\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)],\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)}:=[\sigma_{k}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k),\sigma_{k}+\delta_{\epsilon}(k)]\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}A_{\epsilon}(k)}:=\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)\cup C_{\epsilon}(k)\cup\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)\;;\end{gathered}

(5.6)

the
binding
sets

\begin{gathered}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{\epsilon}^{-}(k)}\,:=\,(\sigma_{k}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k)-\eta_{\epsilon}(k)\,,\,\sigma_{k}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k)),\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{\epsilon}^{+}(k)}\,:=\,(\sigma_{k}+\delta_{\epsilon}(k),\sigma_{k}+\delta_{\epsilon}(k)+\eta_{\epsilon}(k))\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{\epsilon}(k)}\,:=\,B_{\epsilon}^{-}(k)\cup B_{\epsilon}^{+}(k)\cup B_{\epsilon}^{-}(k+1)\cup B_{\epsilon}^{+}(k+1)\end{gathered}

(5.7)

and the
m_{k}
-
neighborhood

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega_{\epsilon}(m_{k})}\,:=\,A_{\epsilon}(k)\cup B_{\epsilon}(k)\;.

(5.8)

This decomposition separates
\Omega_{\epsilon}(m_{k})
into three
types of of sets, see Figure
2
. (i) A slowly growing
sequence of intervals, denoted by
C_{\epsilon}(k)
, that properly
contains
{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})
, on which we know the asymptotic behaviour of

\phi_{\epsilon}
thanks to the local ergodicity results of the previous
subsection. (ii) Shrinking neighbourhoods of the unstable equilibrium
points
\sigma_{k}
, denoted by
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)
. We have no
information on the behaviour of
\phi_{\epsilon}
in the interior of
these sets. In these intervals,
\phi_{\epsilon}
changes from values
close to
0
to values close to
1
. (iii) Shrinking sets, denoted by

B^{\pm}_{\epsilon}(k)
, needed to connect the definition of the
test function in the two previous class of sets.

\sigma_{k}
\sigma_{k+1}
m_{k}
B_{\epsilon}^{-}(k)
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)
B_{\epsilon}^{+}(k)
C_{\epsilon}(k)
{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})
B_{\epsilon}^{-}(k+1)
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)
B_{\epsilon}^{+}(k+1)

Figure 2.
The core and the binding sets.

Fix
\sigma\in{\mathcal{W}}
, and denote by
{\mathscr{L}}_{\sigma,\epsilon}
the
linearization of the generator
{\mathscr{L}}_{\epsilon}
around
\sigma

(centered at the origin and acting on
C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
):

({\mathscr{L}}_{\sigma,\epsilon}\psi)(x)\,=\,x\,{\textsf{b}}^{\prime}(\sigma)\,\psi^{\prime}(x)+\epsilon\,{\textsf{a}}(\sigma)\,\psi^{\prime\prime}(x)\;.

(5.9)

Let
\rho_{\sigma,\epsilon}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
be the solution of
the Poisson equation

{\mathscr{L}}_{\sigma,\epsilon}u=0\,,\quad u(-\infty)=0\,,\quad u(+\infty)=1\;.

A simple computation yields that

\rho_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x)\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}{2\pi\epsilon}}\int_{-\infty}^{x}e^{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)y^{2}/2\epsilon}\,dy\,.

(5.10)

The test function
Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
is defined as

Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}=J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}/{\textsf{a}}
, where

J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
is equal to
\rho_{\sigma_{k},\epsilon}
in the
neighbourhood
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)
of
\sigma_{k}
and as

1-\rho_{\sigma_{k+1},\epsilon}
in
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)
. More
precisely, recall that
B_{\epsilon}(k)
stands for the binding
set. We first define the test function on

{\mathbb{R}}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(k)
. Let

J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\setminus B_{\epsilon}(k)\to{\mathbb{R}}

be given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)}\;:=\;\begin{cases}\rho_{\sigma_{k},\epsilon}(x-\sigma_{k}),&\text{if }x\in\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)\\
1,&\text{if }x\in C_{\epsilon}(k)\\
(1-\rho_{\sigma_{k+1},\epsilon})(x-\sigma_{k+1}),&\text{if }x\in\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)\\
0,&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(5.11)

We extend the definition of
J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
to
B_{\epsilon}(k)

by linear interpolation.

The test function
Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is given
by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}:=J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}/{\textsf{a}}
. It
has compact support, it is continuous, piecewise smooth, and its
derivative is discontinuous at the boundary of
B_{\epsilon}(k)
.

Since
Q_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
is piecewise smooth and continuous,
equation (
5.5
) holds. Multiply both sides of this equation
by
\exp\{S(m_{k})/\epsilon\}/\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}
,
to get that

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx

(5.12)

\displaystyle+\,\epsilon\,\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\,\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{1,k})(x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,G(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx\;.

The resolvent equation

We estimate each term of (
5.12
) separately. By the definition of

Q^{1,k}_{\epsilon}
, and since
{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})\subset C_{\epsilon}(k)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,G(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx

(5.13)

\displaystyle=\;\frac{g({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathscr{E}}(m_{k})}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx\,=\,g({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\pi_{1}(m_{k})\,(1+o_{\epsilon}(1))\,,

where
\pi_{1}(\cdot)
has been introduced in (
2.5
). On the other
hand, since
\phi_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
is uniformly bounded and

J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
vanishes on
\Omega_{\epsilon}(k)^{c}
, by
Proposition
5.2
,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx

(5.14)

\displaystyle=\;\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{\Omega_{\epsilon}(k)}J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\,\lambda\,f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\pi_{1}(m_{k})\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

We turn to the second term on the left-hand side of (
5.12
). Recall
the definition of
\sigma_{1}(j,j+1)
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, introduced in
(
2.5
). Since the test function
J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
vanishes on

\Omega_{\epsilon}(k)^{c}
, the integral can be taken over the set

\Omega_{\epsilon}(m_{k})
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle\epsilon\,\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{\Omega_{\epsilon}(m_{k})}(\,\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{1,k})(x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\,-\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\sum_{i=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}(k,k+i)}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{h^{i}_{k}={\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\}\,[\,f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+i))-f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,]\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

The proof is carried out estimating the integral in each region
forming
\Omega_{\epsilon}(m_{k})
. By definition,

\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
vanishes on
C_{\epsilon}(k)
. Lemma
5.5
takes care of the integral on the set

B_{\epsilon}(k)
, where
J^{1,k}_{\epsilon}
is linear, and Lemma

5.7
on the set

\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)\cup\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)
.

Lemma 5.5
.

For all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\Big|\,\frac{\epsilon\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{B_{\epsilon}(k)}(\,\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{1,k})(x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx\,\Big|\,=\,0\;.

Proof.

To fix ideas, we estimate the integral on the set

B^{-}_{\epsilon}(k)
. By definition of
J^{1,k}_{\epsilon}
,
(
5.10
), (
5.3
), and an elementary Gaussian estimate,

J^{1,k}_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{k}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k))\,=\,\rho_{\sigma_{k},\epsilon}(-\delta_{\epsilon}(k))\,\leq\,\sqrt{\frac{-\,\epsilon}{2\pi S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})}}\,\frac{1}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)}\,e^{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})\delta_{\epsilon}(k)^{2}/2\epsilon}\;.

Therefore, on
B^{-}_{\epsilon}(k)
, since
J^{1,k}_{\epsilon}
has been
defined by linear interpolation, and the length of the binding
interval
B^{-}_{\epsilon}(k)
is
\eta_{\epsilon}(k)
,
\partial_{x}\,J^{1,k}_{\epsilon}
is constant and

\big|\,(\partial_{x}\,J^{1,k}_{\epsilon})(x)\,\big|\,\leq\,\frac{1}{\eta_{\epsilon}(k)}\,\sqrt{\frac{-\,\epsilon}{2\pi S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})}}\,\frac{1}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)}\,e^{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k)^{2}/2\epsilon}\;.

On the other hand, as
\phi_{\epsilon}
belongs to
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\rm loc}
,
an integration by parts yields that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\epsilon}(k)}(\partial_{x}\phi_{\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx\,=

\displaystyle\big[\,\phi_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{r}})-\phi_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{l}})\,\big]\,e^{-[S({\mathtt{r}})-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}

\displaystyle\,+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\int_{B_{\epsilon}(k)}\big[\,\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{l}})\,\big]\,S^{\prime}(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx\;,

where
{\mathtt{l}}
,
{\mathtt{r}}
represent the endpoints of the interval

B_{\epsilon}(k)
. By Proposition
5.2
, as the interval

B_{\epsilon}(k)
is contained in the set
J_{k}
,

\sup_{x\in B_{\epsilon}(k)}|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{l}})|\leq C_{0}\,\sqrt{\epsilon}/\delta_{\epsilon}(k)
for some finite constant
C_{0}

which depends on
g(\cdot)
,
\lambda
,
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,

{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
, but not on
\epsilon
, and which may change from line
to line. Since

|S^{\prime}(x)|=|S^{\prime}(x)-S^{\prime}(\sigma_{k})|\leq C_{0}\,|x-\sigma_{k}|\leq C_{0}\,(\delta_{\epsilon}(k)+\eta_{\epsilon}(k))
, the absolute value of the
previous expression is bounded by

C_{0}\,\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)}\,\Big\{\,1\,+\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\big[\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k)+\eta_{\epsilon}(k)\,\big]\,\eta_{\epsilon}(k)\Big\}\,\sup_{x\in B_{\epsilon}(k)}e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,.

Recall that we have a term

\exp\{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k)^{2}/2\epsilon\}
coming
from the derivative of
J^{1,k}_{\epsilon}
. Since

{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\leq S(\sigma_{k})-S(m_{k})
,

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\,e^{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k)^{2}/2\epsilon}\,\sup_{x\in B_{\epsilon}(k)}e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,\leq\,e^{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k)^{2}/2\epsilon}\,\sup_{x\in B_{\epsilon}(k)}e^{-[S(x)-S(\sigma_{k})]/\epsilon}

As the first derivatives of the functions
{\textsf{a}}(\cdot)
,

{\textsf{b}}(\cdot)
are Lipschitz continuous, and

|x-\sigma_{k}|\leq\delta_{\epsilon}(k)+\eta_{\epsilon}(k)
for all

x\in B^{-}_{\epsilon}(k)
, by a Taylor expansion,

S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k})\,\frac{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)^{2}}{2\epsilon}\,+\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\big\{\,S(\sigma_{k})-S(x)\,\big\}\;\leq\;\frac{C_{0}}{\epsilon}\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k)\,\{\eta_{\epsilon}(k)+\delta_{\epsilon}(k)^{2}\}

for all
x\in B^{-}_{\epsilon}(k)
. By definition of

\delta_{\epsilon}(k)
,
\eta_{\epsilon}(k)
, this quantity is bounded
uniformly in
\epsilon
.

Since
\eta_{\epsilon}(k)\leq\delta_{\epsilon}(k)
, recollecting all
previous estimates, yields that expression appearing in the statement
of the lemma is bounded by

C_{0}\,\sqrt{\epsilon}\;\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)\,\eta_{\epsilon}(k)}\;\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)}\,\Big\{\,1\,+\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k)\,\eta_{\epsilon}(k)\Big\}\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\delta_{\epsilon}(k)}\;,

where we used the definition of
\eta_{\epsilon}(k)
. By definition of

\delta_{\epsilon}(k)
, this quantity vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
, which
completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

One may obtain a much better estimate for

\sup_{x\in B_{\epsilon}(k)}|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{l}})|
. It is enough to repeat the proof of Proposition
5.2

with
m_{k}
,
a
,
b
replaced by
{\mathtt{l}}
,
{\mathtt{l}}
,
\sigma_{k}
,
respectively, and setting

\eta=\sqrt{1-\kappa}\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k)
for some
0<\kappa<1
.
The prof of
[
7
, Theorem 6.3.1]
also provides bounds for the

L^{2}(B_{\epsilon}(k))
norm of
\partial_{x}\phi_{\epsilon}
which can
replace the bound for

\sup_{x\in B_{\epsilon}(k)}|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}({\mathtt{l}})|
used in the previous proof.

We turn to the set
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)\cup\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)
.

Lemma 5.7
.

For all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)\cup\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)}(\,\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{1,k})(x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\,-\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{i=\pm 1}\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}(k,k+i)}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{h^{i}_{k}={\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\}\,\big[\,f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+i))-f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,\big]\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

Proof.

Consider the interval
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)
. A similar argument
applies to
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k)
. Recall from (
5.10
) the
definition of
\rho_{\sigma_{k},\epsilon}
, and that

J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(x)=1-\rho_{\sigma_{k+1},\epsilon}(x-\sigma_{k+1})
on
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)
. Integrate by
parts. The contribution of the boundary terms is given by

-\,\frac{\sqrt{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})}}{2\pi}\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\,\sum_{i=\pm 1}i\,\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{i})\,e^{(1/2\epsilon)\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})\,(\sigma_{k+1}-x_{i})^{2}}e^{(1/\epsilon)(S(m_{k})-S(x_{i}))}\,,

where
x_{i}=\sigma_{k+1}+i\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)
. If

{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}<S(\sigma_{k+1})-S(m_{k})
, this expression vanishes
exponentially fast as
\epsilon\to 0
. On the other hand, if

{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}=S(\sigma_{k+1})-S(m_{k})
, by the definition of

\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)
given in (
5.3
) and a Taylor expansion,

\Big|\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,\big[\,S(\sigma_{k+1})-S(\sigma_{k+1}\pm\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1))\,\big]\,+\,\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})\,\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)^{2}\,\Big|\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\frac{\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)^{3}}{\epsilon}\,=\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

(5.15)

Hence, by Proposition
5.2
which establishes that

\phi_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{k+1}+\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1))
,

\phi_{\epsilon}(\sigma_{k+1}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1))
are close to

\phi_{\epsilon}(m_{k+1})
,
\phi_{\epsilon}(m_{k})
, respectively,
and by the formula for the jump rates presented in (
2.6
),
the expression in the penultimate displayed equation is equal to

\displaystyle-\,(1+o_{\epsilon}(1))\,\frac{\sqrt{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})}}{2\pi}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{h^{+}_{k}={\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\}(\phi_{\epsilon}(m_{k+1})-\phi_{\epsilon}(m_{k}))

\displaystyle=\;-\,(1+o_{\epsilon}(1))\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}(k,k+1)}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{h^{+}_{k}={\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\}\,\big[\,f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k+1))-f_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{1}(k))\,\big]\;.

We turn to the bulk part in the integration by parts formula. It is
given by

-\,\frac{\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}}{\sqrt{2\pi\,\epsilon}}\,\int_{\sigma_{k+1}-\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)}^{\sigma_{k+1}+\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)}\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\big\{\,\epsilon\,(\partial^{2}_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{1,k})(x)\,+\,\frac{{\textsf{b}}(x)}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,(\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{1,k})(x)\,\big\}\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\,dx\;.

(5.16)

This equation explains the construction of the test function

J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}
, as we show below that the expression inside
braces is small. We estimate individually each term of the previous
integral.

By (
4.2
), the solution of the resolvent equation

\phi_{\epsilon}
is bounded by a constant, uniformly in

\epsilon
. By the definition (
5.11
) of

J_{\epsilon}^{1,k}(\cdot)
, and since
S^{\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})=0
,
the expression inside braces is equal
to

\sqrt{\frac{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})}{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\Big\{\,S^{\prime}(x)-S^{\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})\,-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})(x-\sigma_{k+1})\,\Big\}\,e^{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})(x-\sigma_{k+1})^{2}/2\epsilon}\;.

Since
S^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)
is Lipschitz-continuous and

|x-\sigma_{k+1}|\leq\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)
, the expression inside
braces is bounded by
C_{0}\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)^{2}
.

Since
\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\leq\exp\{[S(\sigma_{k+1})-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon\}
, on
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)
,
the Taylor expansion performed in (
5.15
) yields that

\theta_{\epsilon}^{(1)}\,e^{S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma_{k+1})(x-\sigma_{k+1})^{2}/2\epsilon}\,e^{-[S(x)-S(m_{k})]/\epsilon}\;\leq\;e^{C_{0}\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)^{3}/\epsilon}\,\leq\,C_{0}\,.

Since the length of the interval
\Lambda_{\epsilon}(k+1)
is

\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)
, putting together the previous estimates,
yields that the absolute value of the integral (
5.16
) is bounded
by

C_{0}\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\ \,\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)^{3}\;.

By definition of
\delta_{\epsilon}(k+1)
, this expression vanishes as

\epsilon\to 0
. This completes the proof of the assertion.
∎

We have all the elements to prove the convergence of the sequence

f_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

On the one hand, by (
4.2
), the sequence

(f_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
is uniformly bounded. On the other hand,
by (
5.12
), (
5.13
), (
5.14
), Lemma
5.4
, and the
definition (
2.7
) of the generator
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
, any pointwise limit
of the sequence
f_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
is a bounded solution of the
reduced resolvent equation (
3.4
). To complete the proof, it
remains to recall the uniqueness of bounded solutions stated in Lemma

5.8
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3
for
p=1
.

The proof is a straightforward consequence of Propositions
5.2

and
5.3
.
∎

Reduced resolvent equation

As we did not find a reference, we prove in this subsection the
reduced resolvent equation solution uniqueness.

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, and a bounded function

g\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. For every
\lambda>0
, the reduced
resolvent equation (
3.4
) has a unique bounded solution.

Proof.

We consider three cases:
1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
,
p={\mathfrak{q}}
and
S(0)=S(1)
,

p={\mathfrak{q}}
and
S(0)\neq S(1)
. Assume that
1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
. Recall from
(
2.23
) the definition of the generator
{\mathbb{L}}_{p}
, and that the

{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
-closed irreducible classes are the sets

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(j)
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Fix
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Denote by
\pi_{p,j}
the probability measure
defined on the set

\{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k):{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)\subset{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(j)\}
by

\pi_{p,j}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))=\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))/\pi({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(j))
,
where
\pi(\cdot)
has been introduced in (
2.25
). By the
definition of the jump rates
R_{p}
,
\pi_{p,j}
satisfies the detailed
balance conditions. Since
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(j)
is a closed irreducible
class the resolvent equation (
3.4
) is autonomous on this set. As
the set
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(j)
is finite, it is immediate to show that the
solution restricted to
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}(j)
is unique. This characterise
the solution
f
on the closed irreducible classes.

By (
2.22
) jumps are allowed only to nearest-neighbor sets. As each
transient state is surrounded by closed irreducible classes, which are
at finite distance, an elementary recursive argument permits to extend
the solution uniqueness to all
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-transient equivalent classes
between two irreducible classes.

Suppose that
p={\mathfrak{q}}
and
S(1)=S(0)
. By Claim 2.C, stated just
before Theorem
2.8
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k,k\pm 1)}:=R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k\pm 1))>0
for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. We have to show
that the constant function equal to
0
is the unique bounded solution
to (
3.4
) with
g=0
. Denote by
f
a solution. Suppose that

f({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k))\neq 0
. Assume, without loss of generality,
that it is positive. Since
f
is the solution of the resolvent
equation with
g=0
,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k,k+1)\,\{F(k+1)-F(k)\}\,+\,R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k,k-1)\,\{F(k-1)-F(k)\}\,=\,\lambda\,F(k)\;,

(5.17)

where
F(k)=f({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k))
,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k,k\pm 1)=R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k\pm 1))
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Suppose that
F(k-1)\leq F(k)
. In this case, since
F(k)>0
, and

\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
is a reversible measure,

F(k+1)-F(k)\,>\,\frac{R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k,k-1)}{R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k,k+1)}\,\{F(k)-F(k-1)\}\,=\,\frac{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k)\,R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k,k-1)}{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+1)\,R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+1,k)}\,\{F(k)-F(k-1)\}\;.

In particular,
F(k+1)-F(k)>0
. Mind the inequality has been changed
to a strict inequality. Iterating this argument yields that

F(k+j+1)-F(k+j)\,>\,\frac{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k)\,R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+1,k)}{\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+j+1)\,R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+j+1,k+j)}\,\{F(k+1)-F(k)\}\,>\,0

for all
j\geq 0
. Since the jump rates and the measure
\pi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\cdot)
are bounded above by a finite constant and below by a
positive constant,
F(k+j)\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
, which
contradicts the assumption that
f(\cdot)
is bounded.

If
F(k-1)>F(k)
, the same argument yields that
F(k-j)\to\infty

as
j\to\infty
, which completes the proof of the lemma in the case

p={\mathfrak{q}}
and
S(1)=S(0)
.

Suppose that
p={\mathfrak{q}}
and
S(1)\neq S(0)
. To fix ideas, assume
without loss of generality that
S(1)<S(0)
. By Claims 2.A and 2.B stated
just before Theorem
2.8
,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k+1))>0
for all

k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(j-1))=0
for some

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(and therefore, for all
j+n{\mathfrak{u}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
).

Fix
k_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{0}),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{0}-1))=0
. Assume
that
F(k_{0})\neq 0
, say
F(k_{0})>0
. Since
f(\cdot)
is the
solution of the resolvent equation,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{0},k_{0}+1)\,\{F(k_{0}+1)-F(k_{0})\}\,=\,\lambda\,F(k_{0})\,>\,0\;,

(5.18)

so that
F(k_{0}+1)>F(k_{0})
.
Let
k_{1}
the next integer to the right of
k_{0}
such that

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{1}),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{1}-1))=0
.
By the resolvent equation (
5.17
) applied inductively,
F(k)
is
seen to be increasing in the interval
k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{1}
. Hence, as

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{1}),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{1}-1))=0
, by
the bound (
5.18
) for
k_{1}
instead of
k_{0}
,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{1},k_{1}+1)\,\{F(k_{1}+1)-F(k_{1})\}\,=\,\lambda\,F(k_{1})\,\geq\,\lambda\,F(k_{0})\;.

Adding the estimates, as
k_{1}\geq k_{0}+1
, yields that

F(k_{1}+1)-F(k_{0})\,\geq\,\lambda\,\Big\{\frac{1}{R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{1},k_{1}+1)}+\frac{1}{R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{0},k_{0}+1)}\,\Big\}F(k_{0})\;.

Thus,
F(k_{0}+j)\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
, in contradiction with the
boundedness of
f(\cdot)
.

If
F(k_{0})=0
for all
k_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{0}),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{0}-1))=0
. By
(
5.18
),
F(k_{0}+1)=0
. Then, by (
5.17
) for
k=k_{0}+1
,

F(k_{0}+2)=0
. By induction, one concludes that
F(k)=0
for all

k\geq k_{0}
. By Postulate
{\mathcal{P}}_{9}({\mathfrak{q}})
, there exists a sequence

(k_{j}:j\geq 1)
such that
k_{j}\to-\infty
,

R_{{\mathfrak{q}}}({\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{j}),{\mathscr{M}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(k_{j}-1))=0
. This
completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

6.
Proof of Theorem
3.3
for
p>1

Following the approach for the first time scale presented in the
previous section, we prove Theorem
3.3
in two steps. We first
show that the solution of the resolvent equation (
1.6
) is
asymptotically constant in each well and then that all limit points
are solution of the reduced resolvent equation (
3.4
).

The proof is similar to the one presented in the previous section,
with one major difference. While for
p=1
, as soon as the diffusion
crossed the saddle point, we very high probability it lands in a new
well. This is not the case in general for
p>1
, since there might be

S
-local minima separating two wells. Hence, crossing a saddle
point, the diffusion may land in a local minima which does not belong
to a level
p
well. In particular, it may return to the original well
before hitting a new well. This possibility requires new estimates to
compute the magnitude of the transition times between two wells, see
Lemma
6.6
and Figure
3
.

Throughout this section,
p\in\{2,\dots,{\mathfrak{q}}\}
and a bounded
function
g\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are fixed. Recall that we denote
by
\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\cdot)
the solution of the resolvent equation
(
1.6
). By (
4.2
) and the definition of
G
,

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)|\,\leq\,\frac{1}{\lambda}\,\max_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\big|\,g({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big|\;.

(6.1)

Local Ergodicity

Recall from (
A.5
) the definition of
\sigma^{p,\pm}_{j,j+1}
,

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. For
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}}\,:=\,\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}(\epsilon)\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{2\,\epsilon\log\epsilon^{-1}}{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})}}\;,\hskip 14.22636pt{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\delta^{p,+}_{k,k+1}}\,:=\,\delta^{p,+}_{k,k+1}(\epsilon)\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{2\,\epsilon\log\epsilon^{-1}}{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k,k+1})}}\;\cdot

(6.2)

Recall from (
2.19
) that we denote by
m^{p,-}_{k}
and

m^{p,+}_{k}
the leftmost and rightmost minima of
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
,
respectively. Denote by
J^{p}_{k},J^{p,+}_{k},J^{p,-}_{k}
the
subintervals of
{\mathbb{R}}
given by

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J^{p,-}_{k}}\,:=\,[\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k}+\delta^{p,+}_{k-1,k}\,,\,m^{+}_{p,k}]\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J^{p,+}_{k}}\,:=\,[m^{-}_{p,k}\,,\,\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}]\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J^{p}_{k}}\,:=\,J^{p,-}_{k}\cup J^{p,+}_{k}\;.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exists a finite constants
C_{0}
,
\epsilon_{0}>0
depending only
on
S(\cdot)
, such that

\sup_{x\in J^{p}_{k}}|\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{p,\epsilon}(m)|\,\leq\,2\,\|g\|_{\infty}\,\Big\{\,\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{p-1}}{\epsilon\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{p}}\,+\,\frac{C_{0}}{\lambda}\,\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\delta^{p,+}_{k-1,k}\wedge\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}}\,\,\Big\}

for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
. In this formula,

{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}
is the constant introduced in Lemma
4.2
.

Proof.

Fix
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and assume that
x\in J^{p,+}_{k}
. The same argument
applies to
x\in J^{p,-}_{k}
. The proof relies on Proposition
4.1

with
a=m^{-}_{p,k}
,
b=\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
,
F=G
and

\vartheta=\theta^{p}_{\epsilon}
. We need to estimate the two terms on
the right-hand side of (
4.3
).

We claim that there exists a finite constant
C_{0}
, independent of

\epsilon
, and
\epsilon_{0}>0
. such that

\sup_{x\in J^{p,+}_{k}}{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\;<\;\tau(m^{-}_{p,k})\,\big]\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}}

(6.3)

for all
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
.

To prove this assertion, recall from Lemma
4.8
that

\sup_{x\in J^{p,+}_{k}}{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\;<\;\tau(m^{-}_{p,k})\,\big]\,\leq\,\frac{1}{\eta}\,\exp\Big\{\,\big[\,\sup_{z\in[m^{-}_{p,k},x]}S(z)-\inf_{y\in[\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\eta,\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}]}S(y)\big]/\epsilon\,\Big\}

for all
x\in J^{p,+}_{k}
because
x-a\leq 1
. Here,

\eta=(1/\sqrt{2})\,\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
. By Lemma

A.5
, for
\epsilon
sufficiently small (so that

\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
is small),

\sup_{z\in[m^{-}_{p,k}\,,\,\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}]}S(z)\,=\,S(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\;.

As
S(\cdot)
in increasing on

[\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\eta,\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}]
, for
\epsilon

sufficiently small, the left-hand side of the penultimate displayed
equation is less than or equal to

\frac{1}{\eta}\,\exp\Big\{\,\Big[S(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\,-\,S(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\eta)\Big]/\epsilon\,\Big\}\;.

By definition of
\eta
, and a second order Taylor expansion, as
S^{\prime\prime}

is Lipschitz-continuous and periodic, the previous expression is less
than or equal to

\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}}\,\exp\Big\{\,\Big[\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\,(\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})^{2}\,+\,C_{0}\,(\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})^{3}\,\Big]/4\epsilon\,\Big\}

for some finite constant
C_{0}
independent of
\epsilon
, whose value
may change from line to line. By definition of
\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
,
this expression is bounded by

C_{0}\,\sqrt{\epsilon}/\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
. This proves
assertion (
6.3
).

We claim that

\frac{1}{\theta^{p}_{\epsilon}}\,\sup_{x\in J^{p,+}_{k}}{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}[\,\tau(m^{-}_{p,k},\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\,]\,\leq\,\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}\,\theta^{p-1}_{\epsilon}}{\epsilon\,\theta^{p}_{\epsilon}}\;,

(6.4)

where
{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}
is the constant appearing in Lemma
4.2
.

By Lemma
4.2
, the left-hand side of (
6.4
) is bounded by

\frac{{\mathfrak{c}}_{0}}{\epsilon\,\theta^{p}_{\epsilon}}\,\exp\Big\{\,\sup_{m^{-}_{p,k}\leq x\leq y\leq\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}}[S(x)-S(y)]/\epsilon\,\Big\}\;.

Let
m^{*}
be the rightmost local minima of
S(\cdot)
smaller than

\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
so that
S(\cdot)
is monotone on the interval

[m^{*},\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}]
. We may restrict the supremum in
x
to
values in the interval
[m^{-}_{p,k},m^{*}]
because for

x\in[m^{*},\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}]
,
S(x)-S(y)\leq 0
if

x\leq y\leq\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
.

Since
S(\cdot)
is monotone between its extremal points, the supremum
appearing in the previous formula is achieved on the extremal points
of
S(\cdot)
:

\sup_{m^{-}_{p,k}\leq x\leq y\leq\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}}[S(x)-S(y)]\,=\,\max_{\sigma\in[m^{-}_{p,k},m^{*}]\cap{\mathcal{W}}}\;\;\max_{m\in[\sigma,m^{*}]\cap{\mathcal{M}}}[S(\sigma)-S(m)]\;.

By Lemma
A.9
, this expression is bounded by
{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}
. This
proves (
6.4
) since

\theta^{p-1}_{\epsilon}=e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}/\epsilon}
, and completes the
proof of the proposition.
∎

By the previous result and the definition of
\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
,

\delta^{p,+}_{k-1,k}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\max_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\sup_{x,y\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}|\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{p,\epsilon}(y)|=0\,.

(6.5)

Convergence to
f_{p}
.

Let
f_{p,\epsilon}\colon{\mathscr{S}}_{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}\,:=\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(m^{-}_{p,k})\;.

(6.6)

The main result of this section reads as follows.

Proposition 6.2
.

For all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\big|\,f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,-\,f_{p}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big|\,=\,0\;,

where
f_{p}(\cdot)
is the unique bounded solution of the
reduced resolvent equation (
3.4
).

Recall the notation in introduced in (
A.5
), and the definition of

\delta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}
presented in (
6.2
). Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\eta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}}\,:=\,\frac{\epsilon}{\delta^{p,+}_{k,k+1}}\;=\;\sqrt{\frac{-\,\epsilon\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1})}{2\,\log\epsilon^{-1}}}\;,\quad k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\;.

Define the
core
sets
C_{p,k}
,
\Lambda^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}
,

A_{p,k}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, [whose dependence on
\epsilon
has been
omitted from the notation] by

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{p,k}}\,:=\,\big[\,\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k}+\delta^{p,+}_{k-1,k}+\eta^{p,+}_{k-1,k}\,,\,\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\eta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}\,\big]\;,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Lambda^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}}\,:=\,\big[\,\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}\,,\,\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}\,\big]\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Lambda_{p,k}}\,:=\,\Lambda^{p,+}_{k-1,k}\cup\Lambda^{p,-}_{k,k+1}\,,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}A_{p,k}}\;:=\;\Lambda^{p,+}_{k-1,k}\,\cup\,C_{p,k}\,\cup\,\Lambda^{p,-}_{k,k+1}\;;

the
binding
sets
B_{l}(\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1})
,

B_{r}(\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1})
,

B_{p,k}
, by

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{l}(\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1})}\,:=\,(\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}-\eta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}\,,\,\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1})\;,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{r}(\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1})}\,:=\,(\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}\,,\,\sigma^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1}+\eta^{p,\pm}_{k,k+1})\;,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}B_{p,k}}\,:=\,B_{l}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k})\,\cup\,B_{r}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k})\,\cup\,B_{l}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\,\cup\,B_{r}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\;;

and the
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
-neighbourhood
\Omega_{p,k}
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega_{p,k}}\,:=\,A_{p,k}\,\cup\,B_{p,k}\;.

(6.7)

We refer to Figure
2
for an illustration.

Recall the definition of the solution of the linearised Poisson
equation
\rho_{\sigma,\epsilon}
, introduced in (
5.10
). The
test function
Q_{\epsilon}^{p,k}
is defined as

Q_{\epsilon}^{p,k}=J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}/{\textsf{a}}
, where

J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}
is equal to

\rho_{\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k},\epsilon}
in the neighbourhood

\Lambda^{p,+}_{k-1,k}
of
\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k}
and as

1-\rho_{\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1},\epsilon}
in

\Lambda^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
. More precisely, we first define the test
function on
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus B_{p,k}
. Let

J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\setminus B_{p,k}\to{\mathbb{R}}

be given by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}(x)}\;:=\;\begin{cases}\rho_{\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k},\epsilon}(x-\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k}),&\text{if }x\in\Lambda^{p,+}_{k-1,k}\\
1,&\text{if }x\in C_{p,k}\\
(1-\rho_{\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1},\epsilon})(x-\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}),&\text{if }x\in\Lambda^{p,-}_{k,k+1}\\
0,&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(6.8)

We extend the definition of
J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}
to
B_{p,k}

by linear interpolation.

The test function
Q_{\epsilon}^{p,k}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is given
by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Q_{\epsilon}^{p,k}:=J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}/{\textsf{a}}
. It
is continuous, piecewise smooth, and its derivative is discontinuous
at the boundary of
B_{p,k}
.

Since
Q_{\epsilon}^{p,k}
is piecewise smooth and continuous,
equation (
5.5
) holds with the index
1
replaced by
p
. Multiply
both sides of this equation by

\exp\{S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))/\epsilon\}/\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}
to get that

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)\,J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx

(6.9)

\displaystyle+\,\epsilon\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}(\,\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{p,k})(x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{p,\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,G(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx\;.

The resolvent equation

We estimate each term of (
6.9
) separately. We start with the
right-hand side. Recall the definition of
\pi_{p}(k)
introduced in
(
2.21
). Since the support of

J^{p,k}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)\,G(\cdot)
is contained in

{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
, where
G(\cdot)
takes the value
g({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))

and
J^{p,k}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
is equal to
1
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,G(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx

(6.10)

\displaystyle=\;\frac{g({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\,(1+o_{\epsilon}(1))\,g({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\pi_{p}(k)\,.

On the other hand, since
\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\cdot)

J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}(\cdot)
are uniformly bounded,

J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}
vanishes on
\Omega_{p,k}^{c}
, and the measure

\exp\{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon\}\,dx
is concentrated on
the global minima of
S(\cdot)
on
\Omega_{p,k}
, by
Lemma
6.1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx

(6.11)

\displaystyle=\;\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{\Omega_{p,k}}J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}(x)\,\frac{1}{{\textsf{a}}(x)}\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\;(1+o_{\epsilon}(1))\,\lambda\,f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\pi_{p}(k)\,,

where
f_{p,\epsilon}(\cdot)
has been introduced in (
6.6
).

We turn to the second term on the left-hand side of (
6.9
) which
is the most delicate one. Since the test function

J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}
vanishes on
\Omega_{p,k}^{c}
, the integral can be
taken over the set
\Omega_{p,k}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

For each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle\epsilon\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{\Omega_{p,k}}(\,\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{p,k})(x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{p,\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx

\displaystyle=\,-\,\sum_{i=\pm 1}R_{p}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k),{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+i))\,[\,f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+i))-f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,]\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

Mind that the right-hand side may vanish since the jump rates may
be equal to
0
. The proof is carried out estimating the integral in each
region forming
\Omega_{p,k}
. By definition,

\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{p,k}
vanishes on

C_{p,k}(\epsilon)
. Lemma
6.4
takes care of the integral on
the set
B_{p,k}
, where
J^{p,k}_{\epsilon}
is linear, and Lemma

6.5
on the set

\Lambda_{p,k}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

For all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\Big|\,\frac{\epsilon\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{B_{p,k}}(\,\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{p,k})(x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{p,\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx\,\Big|\,=\,0\;.

The proof of this result is omitted being similar to the one of
Lemma
5.5
. We turn to the set
\Lambda_{p,k}
. Recall the
definition of the weight
\sigma_{p}(j,j+1)
,
j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, introduced
in (
2.21
).

Lemma 6.5
.

For all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon\,\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}}{\sqrt{2\pi\epsilon}}\,\int_{\Lambda_{p,k}}(\,\partial_{x}\,J_{\epsilon}^{p,k})(x)\,(\partial_{x}\phi_{p,\epsilon})(x)\,e^{-[S(x)-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))]/\epsilon}\,dx

(6.12)

\displaystyle\,=

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\frac{1}{\sigma_{p}(k,k+1)}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{\,h^{p,+}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}\,\}\,[\,f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1))-f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,]

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\frac{1}{\sigma_{p}(k-1,k)}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{\,h^{p,-}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}\,\}\,[\,f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k-1))-f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,]\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
5.7
yields that the left-hand side of
(
6.12
) is equal to

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\sqrt{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{\,h^{p,+}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}\,\}\,[\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})-\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\,]

(6.13)

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\sqrt{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k})}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{\,h^{p,-}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}\,\}\,[\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k}-\delta^{p,+}_{k-1,k})-\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k}+\delta^{p,+}_{k-1,k})\,]\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

By Lemma
6.1
, we may replace in the previous formula,

\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})
and

\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k}+\delta^{p,+}_{k-1,k})
by

f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
at a cost
o_{\epsilon}(1)
. Hence, by Lemma

6.6
below and (
6.14
), the previous expression is equal to

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\sqrt{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})}\,\varpi^{+}_{p,k}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{\,h^{p,+}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}\,\}[\,f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1))-f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,]

\displaystyle-\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\sqrt{-\,S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k})}\,\varpi^{-}_{p,k}\,{\mathtt{1}}\{\,h^{p,-}_{k}\,=\,{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}\,\}[\,f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k-1))-f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,]\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

To complete the proof of the lemma, it remains to recall the
definition of
\varpi^{\pm}_{p,k}
.
∎

Recall from (
2.20
) that we denote by
{\mathscr{W}}^{(p)}_{j,j+1}
,

j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, the finite set where
S(\cdot)
attains its global
maximum in the interval
[m^{+}_{p,j},,m^{-}_{p,j+1}]
. In the next
lemma, we express

\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})
as a convex
combination of
f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
and

f_{p,\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1))
. Mind that
\varpi^{+}_{p,k}
,
defined in the next lemma, is equal to
1
if
{\mathscr{W}}^{(p)}_{k,k+1}
is
a singleton,

{\mathscr{W}}^{(p)}_{k,k+1}=\{\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}\}=\{\sigma^{p,+}_{k,k+1}\}
, that is if

\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}=\sigma^{p,+}_{k,k+1}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Fix
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and suppose that

S(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))={\mathfrak{h}}_{p}
. Then,

\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\,=\,(1-\varpi^{+}_{p,k})\,f^{(p)}_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,+\,\varpi^{+}_{p,k}\,f^{(p)}_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k+1))\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;,

where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varpi^{+}_{p,k}}\,:=\,\frac{1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})}}{\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathscr{W}}^{(p)}_{k,k+1}}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}}\;.

Proof.

If
\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}=\sigma^{p,+}_{k,k+1}
, the assertion
follows from Lemma
6.1
. Assume, therefore that

\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}<\sigma^{p,+}_{k,k+1}
. The proof is based on
the second assertion of Proposition
4.1
. Let

x=x_{\epsilon}=\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
,

a=a_{\epsilon}=\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1}
,

b=b_{\epsilon}=\sigma^{p,+}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,+}_{k,k+1}
. Since

S(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1})-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))={\mathfrak{h}}_{p}
, by Lemmata

4.2
and
A.11
,

{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau(a,b)\,\big]/\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,=\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

By the proof of Corollary
4.9
,

{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\{\tau(b)<\tau(a)\}\,=\,\varpi^{+}_{p,k}\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

Note that we can not apply directly Corollary
4.9
as the initial
point depends on
\epsilon
. But the proof goes through without
problem.

It follows from the previous estimates and the second
assertion of Proposition
4.1
that

\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}+\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\,=\,(1-\varpi^{+}_{p,k})\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,-}_{k,k+1}-\delta^{p,-}_{k,k+1})\,+\,\varpi^{+}_{p,k}\,\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{-}_{p,k+1}+\delta^{+}_{p,k+1})\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;.

To complete the proof of the lemma, it remains to call Lemma
6.1
.
∎

Similarly, if
S(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k})-S({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))={\mathfrak{h}}_{p}
, then,

\phi_{p,\epsilon}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k}-\delta^{p,+}_{k-1,k})\,=\,(1-\varpi^{-}_{p,k})\,f^{(p)}_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,+\,\varpi^{-}_{p,k}\,f^{(p)}_{\epsilon}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k-1))\,+\,o_{\epsilon}(1)\;,

(6.14)

where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varpi^{-}_{p,k}}\,:=\,\frac{1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma^{p,+}_{k-1,k})}}{\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathscr{W}}^{(p)}_{k-1,k}}1/\sqrt{-S^{\prime\prime}(\sigma)}}\;.

7.
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We follow the strategy proposed in
[
20
]
and applied in

[
18
,
19
]
in the context of multidimensional diffusion
processes. We start presenting a consequence of Theorem
3.3
.

Metastability

Fix
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, and denote by
Y^{(p)}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
the
process
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
speeded-up by
\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}
:

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Y^{(p)}_{\epsilon}(t):=X_{\epsilon}(t\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon})
. Denote by

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the probability
measure on
D({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
induced by the process

Y^{(p)}_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
starting from
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Note that

{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}={\mathbb{P}}^{\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon},\epsilon}_{x}
, where
{\mathbb{P}}^{\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon},\epsilon}_{x}
has been
introduced at the beginning of Section
4
.

Fix a subset
A
of
{\mathbb{R}}
and a trajectory

{\mathtt{x}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Denote by

{\textsf{T}}_{A}(t)={\textsf{T}}_{{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot),A}(t)
,
t\geq 0
, the total time
the trajectory
{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot)
spent in
A
in the time interval

[0,t]
, and by
{\textsf{S}}_{A}(\cdot):={\textsf{S}}_{{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot),A}(\cdot)

the generalised inverse of
{\textsf{T}}_{A}(\cdot)
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textsf{T}}_{A}(t)}\,:=\,\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{{}_{A}}({\mathtt{x}}(s))\,ds\;,\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\textsf{S}}_{A}(t)}\,:=\,\sup\{s\geq 0:{\textsf{T}}_{A}(s)\leq t\}\;,

where, recall,
\chi_{{}_{B}}
,
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, represents the indicator
function of the set
B
.

Let
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}({\mathscr{F}}_{t}:t\geq 0)
be the augmentation of the
natural filtration on
{\mathbb{C}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
generated by

{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot)
. For each
t\geq 0
,
A\subset{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\textsf{S}}_{A}(t)
is
a stopping time with respect to this filtration. Let

({\mathtt{x}}^{{\textsf{T}},A}(t):t\geq 0)
be the process

{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot)
time-changed by

({\textsf{S}}_{A}(r):r\geq 0)
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{x}}^{{\textsf{T}},A}(t)}\,:=\,{\mathtt{x}}({\textsf{S}}_{A}(t))\;.

(7.1)

Clearly,
{\mathtt{x}}^{{\textsf{T}},A}(\cdot)
is an
A
-valued trajectory
adapted to the filtration

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathscr{G}}^{{\textsf{T}},A}_{r}:={\mathscr{F}}_{{\textsf{S}}_{A}(r)}
,
r\geq 0
. We call
{\mathtt{x}}^{{\textsf{T}},A}(\cdot)
the
trace process
of

{\mathtt{x}}(\cdot)
on
A
.

Recall from Corollary
4.7
the definition of the set
{\mathscr{E}}_{p}
,

1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Define the projection
\Psi_{p}\colon{\mathscr{E}}_{p}\to{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Psi_{p}(x)}\,:=\,\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)\,\chi_{{}_{{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}}(x)\;.

Define the
{\mathscr{S}}_{p}
-valued
order process

{\mathtt{y}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p}_{\epsilon}\colon C({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})\to D({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathscr{S}}_{p})
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{y}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p}(t)}\,:=\,\Psi_{p}({\mathtt{x}}^{{\textsf{T}},{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}(t))\;.

Denote by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}
,

x\in{\mathscr{E}}_{p}
, the probability measure on
D({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathscr{E}}_{p})

obtained as the push-forward of
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}
by

{\mathtt{x}}^{{\textsf{T}},{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}
(understood as a function from

C({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathbb{R}})
to
D({\mathbb{R}}_{+},{\mathscr{E}}_{p})
). Let

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{Q}}_{x}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}
be the push-forward
of
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}
by
\Psi_{p}
, which corresponds
to the distribution of
{\mathtt{y}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p}(\cdot)
under

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}
. Recall the definition of the
measure
{\mathtt{Q}}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}^{(p)}
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, introduced below
(
3.3
). Next result follows from Theorem
3.3
and

[
21
, Theorem 2.3]
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
. Then,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}\Big[\,\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{{}_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}}({\mathtt{x}}(s))\,ds\,\Big]\,=\,0

(7.2)

for every
t\geq 0
. Furthermore, for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and sequences

x_{\epsilon}\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
,
{{\mathtt{Q}}}_{x_{\epsilon}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}
converges to
{{\mathtt{Q}}}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}^{(p)}
.

Next assertion is a consequence of (
7.2
). We refer to

[
20
, display (3.2)]
for a proof.

Corollary 7.2
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
. Then,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,{\textsf{S}}_{{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}(t)>t+\delta\,\big]\,=\,0

for all
t>0
and
\delta>0
.

The next result is a consequence of the previous theorem. It can also
be obtained from Theorem
3.3
, see
[
21
, Lemma 4.2]
.

Corollary 7.3
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
. Then, for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,

\lim_{a\to 0}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{E}}_{p}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)))\leq a\,\big]\,=\,0\;.

Corollary 7.4
.

Fix
1\leq p<{\mathfrak{q}}
. Then,

\lim_{t\to\infty}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1})>t\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,\big]\,=\,0\;.

Proof.

We proceed in two steps. We first apply Lemma
4.3
to show that
the process
X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
reaches
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
in a time of order

\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}
, and then use the convergence of the order
process to show that starting from
{\mathscr{M}}_{p}
we reach a

{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-closed irreducible class in a time of order

t\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}
for
t
large.

By Lemma
4.3
and Chebyshev inequality,

{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1})>t\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,\big]\,\leq\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})\leq\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,,\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1})>t\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,\big]\,+\,R_{\epsilon}(x)\,,

where

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}|R_{\epsilon}(x)|\,=\,0\;.

(7.3)

If
t>1
, by the strong Markov property, the first term on the
right-hand side is bounded by

\sup_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}}{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{m}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1})\geq(t-1)\,\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,\big]\,=\,\sup_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}}{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{m}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p+1})\geq t-1\,\big]\;.

By Lemma
4.2
, we may replace in the previous equation

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}
,
t-1
by
{\mathscr{E}}_{p+1}
,
t-2
, respectively, at an
extra cost
R^{(1)}_{\epsilon}(m)
satisfying (
7.3
) (with

\sup_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}}
replacing
\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}
). (We are using
here that once
{\mathscr{E}}_{p+1}
is reached,
{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}
is attained
immediately after in view of Lemma
4.2
). By (
7.2
), the
previous probability (with
{\mathscr{E}}_{p+1}
,
t-2
replacing

{\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}
,
t-1
, respectively) is less than or equal to

{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{m}\Big[\,\int_{0}^{t-2}\chi_{{}_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}}({\mathtt{x}}(s))\,ds\leq 1\,,\,\tau({\mathscr{E}}_{p+1})\geq t-2\,\Big]\,+\,R^{(2)}_{\epsilon}(m)\,.

where
R^{(2)}_{\epsilon}(m)
fulfills (
7.3
). By definition of the
trace process and the measure
{\mathbb{P}}^{{\textsf{T}},p,\epsilon}_{m}
,
introduced just before the statement of the Theorem
7.1
, the
previous probability is bounded by

{\mathbb{P}}^{{\textsf{T}},p,\epsilon}_{m}\big[\,\tau({\mathscr{E}}_{p+1})\geq t-3\,\big]\,.

Since the first time the trace process
{\mathtt{x}}^{{\textsf{T}},p}(\cdot)

hits
{\mathscr{E}}_{p+1}
corresponds to the first time the order process

{\mathtt{y}}^{{\textsf{T}},p}(\cdot)
hits a recurrent element, the previous
expression is equal to

{\mathtt{Q}}^{{\textsf{T}},p,\epsilon}_{m}\big[\,\tau({\mathfrak{R}}_{p})\geq t-3\,\big]\,,

where
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}=\cup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}(k)
, and, recall from
(
2.13
),
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}(k)
are the
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}
-recurrent classes.
By construction,
{\mathfrak{R}}_{p}={\mathscr{M}}_{p+1}
.

Since the set
\{\tau({\mathfrak{R}}_{p})\geq t-3\}
is closed for the Skorohod
topology, by Theorem
7.1
,

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{m\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}}{\mathtt{Q}}^{{\textsf{T}},p,\epsilon}_{m}\big[\,\tau({\mathfrak{R}}_{p})\geq t-3\,\big]\,\leq\,\sup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathtt{Q}}^{(p)}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}\big[\,\tau({\mathfrak{R}}_{p})\geq t-3\,\big]\,.

As
p<{\mathfrak{q}}
the process
{\mathbb{X}}_{p}(\cdot)
has recurrent classes.
Since the rates are periodic, this expression vanishes as

t\to\infty
, uniformly in
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

Finite-dimensional distributions

The proof of Theorem
3.2
is based on the next result, whose
proof is similar to the one of
[
20
, Proposition 2.1]
,

[
18
, Proposition 10.2]
. Recall from the beginning of this
section the definition of the measure
{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}
.

Proposition 7.5
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
r_{0}>0
as in (
3.2
), and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then,

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\mathbb{P}_{x_{\epsilon}}^{p,\epsilon}\Big[\,\bigcap_{j=1}^{{{\mathfrak{n}}}}\big\{\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{j,\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{j}))\,\big\}\,\Big]\,=\,{\mathtt{Q}}^{(p)}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)}\big[\,\bigcap_{j=1}^{{{\mathfrak{n}}}}\big\{\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{j})={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{j})\,\}\,\,\big]

for all
{{\mathfrak{n}}}\geq 1
,
0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{{{\mathfrak{n}}}}
,

k_{1},\dots,k_{{\mathfrak{n}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and sequences

x_{\epsilon}\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
,
t_{j,\epsilon}\to t_{j}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of
[
20
, Proposition 2.1]
.
Assume that
{\mathfrak{n}}=1
. The case
{\mathfrak{n}}>1
is obtained by induction
using the Markov property of the diffusion process

X_{\epsilon}(\cdot)
.

Fix
t>0
,
j
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and a sequence
x_{\epsilon}\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))
. By Theorem
7.1
, and the definition of the measure

{\mathtt{Q}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}_{x}
,

\displaystyle{{\mathtt{Q}}}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j)}^{(p)}[{\mathtt{x}}(t)={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)]

\displaystyle=\;\lim_{\delta\to 0}{{\mathtt{Q}}}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j)}^{(p)}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t-3\delta)={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)\,]

\displaystyle=\;\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathtt{Q}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}_{x_{\epsilon}}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t-3\delta)={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)\,]

\displaystyle=\;\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{P}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}_{x_{\epsilon}}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t-3\delta)\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,]\;.

Fix
t>0
and a sequence
t_{\epsilon}\to t
. By Lemma
7.6
below,

{{\mathtt{Q}}}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j)}^{(p)}[{\mathtt{x}}(t)={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)]\;\leq\;\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x_{\epsilon}}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{\epsilon})\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)\,]\;.

(7.4)

Suppose that the inequality is strict for some
k_{0}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Then, there exists
\eta>0
such that

{{\mathtt{Q}}}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j)}^{(p)}[{\mathtt{x}}(t)={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{0})]\,+\,2\eta\;\leq\;\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x_{\epsilon}}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{\epsilon})\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{0})\,]\;.

Choose a finite subset
A
of
{\mathbb{Z}}
which contains
k_{0}
and such
that

1-\eta\;\leq\;\sum_{k\in A}{{\mathtt{Q}}}_{{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j)}^{(p)}[{\mathtt{x}}(t)={\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)]\;.

By the previous estimates,

1-\eta\;\leq\;\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\sum_{k\in A}{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x_{\epsilon}}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{\epsilon})\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)\,]\,-\,2\eta\;\leq\;1-2\eta\;,

which is a contradiction. Therefore, we have an identity in
(
7.4
) for each
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. If the
\liminf
does not coincide
with the
\limsup
, we may repeat the same argument to obtain a
contradiction. Thus, the
\liminf
in (
7.4
) is actually a limit
and the inequality is an identity. This completes the proof of the
proposition.
∎

The next result is stated in
[
20
, Lemma 3.1]
in the context of
discrete-valued Markov processes. It estimates the probability that
the trace process
Y^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p}_{\epsilon}(t)
belongs to a set

{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))
by the probability that the original process

Y^{p}_{\epsilon}(t)
belongs to this set at the price of changing the
time
t
by
t-a
for
a
small.

Lemma 7.6
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, and
j
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then,

{\mathbb{P}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t-3b)\in\mathcal{E}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big]\,\leq\,{\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t)\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,]\,+\,R_{\epsilon}(x,t,b)\;,

where

\lim_{b\rightarrow 0}\,\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))}|\,R_{\epsilon}(x,t_{\epsilon},b)\,|\,=\,0

for all
t>0
, and sequences
t_{\epsilon}\to t
.

Proof.

Fix
t>0
,
j
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
x\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))
, a sequence

t_{\epsilon}\to t
, and
2<\gamma<3
. By the definition (
7.1
) of
the trace process and the trivial fact that
S_{\epsilon}(t)\geq t
,
for
b\in(0,t/3)

{\mathbb{P}}^{{{\mathtt{T}}},p,\epsilon}_{x}\big[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t-3b)\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big]\,=\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}\big[\,{\mathtt{x}}(S_{\epsilon}(t-3b))\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big]\;\leq\;{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,A_{\epsilon}(t,b)\,]\,+\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,B_{\epsilon}(t,b)\,]\;,

where

\displaystyle A_{\epsilon}(t,\,b)\,=\,\{\,S_{\epsilon}(t-3b)>t-\gamma b\,\}\;,

\displaystyle B_{\epsilon}(t,b)\,=\,\{\,{\mathtt{x}}(s)\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\;\;\text{for some}\ s\in[t-3b,t-\gamma b]\,\}\ .

By Corollary
7.2
, as
\gamma<3
,

\limsup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}_{p}}\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,A_{\epsilon}(t,b)\,]=0\ .

On the other hand,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[B_{\epsilon}(t,b)]\,\leq\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}\big[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{\epsilon})\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big]\,+\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}\big[\,B_{\epsilon}(t,b)\,,\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{\epsilon})\notin{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big]

for any
t_{\epsilon}\geq 0
.

It remains to prove that

\limsup_{b\rightarrow 0}\,\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))}{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}\big[\,B_{\epsilon}(t,b)\,,\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{\epsilon})\notin{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big]\,=\,0

if
t_{\epsilon}
is a sequence converging to
t
. By Corollary

7.3
, the definition of
B_{\epsilon}(t,b)
, and the strong
Markov property,

\limsup_{b\rightarrow 0}\,\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))}\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}\big[\,B_{\epsilon}(t,b)\,,\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{\epsilon})\in{\mathscr{E}}_{p}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))\,\big]\,=\,0\;.

On the other hand, as
\gamma>2
, for
\epsilon
sufficiently small,

t_{\epsilon}-s\in[2b,4b]
for all
s\in[t-3b,t-\gamma b]
. Hence,
by the strong Markov property and Lemma
7.7
,

\limsup_{b\rightarrow 0}\,\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(j))}\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,B_{\epsilon}(t,b)\,,\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{\epsilon})\notin{\mathscr{E}}_{p}\,]\;=\;0\;.

The assertion of the lemma follows from the previous estimates.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Fix
1\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, and
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then,

\limsup_{b\rightarrow 0}\,\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\sup_{t\in[2b,4b]}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))}\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t)\notin{\mathscr{E}}_{p}\,]\;=\;0\;.

Proof.

We actually prove a stronger statement, in which the well

{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k))
, and the time interval
[2b,4b]
are replaced by

{\mathbb{R}}
,
[c,\infty)
, respectively: for all
c>0

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\sup_{t\geq c}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t)\notin{\mathscr{E}}_{p}\,]\;=\;0\;.

(7.5)

We proceed by induction in
p
. Assume that
p=1
, and let

\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}
be a time-scale such that

1\prec\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\prec\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}
. Then,
for all
t>0
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
, by the Markov property at time

t\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}-\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t)\notin{\mathscr{E}}_{1}\,]\,=\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{\epsilon}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t\theta^{(1)}_{\epsilon})\notin{\mathscr{E}}_{1}\,]\,\leq\,\sup_{y\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbb{P}}_{y}^{\epsilon}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon})\notin{\mathscr{E}}_{1}\,]\;.

By the strong Markov property at time
\tau({\mathcal{M}})
, for any

y\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the previous probability is bounded by

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathbb{P}}_{y}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathcal{M}})>\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\,]\,+\,{\mathbb{P}}_{y}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathcal{M}})\leq\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\,,\,{\mathtt{x}}(\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon})\notin{\mathscr{E}}_{1}\,]

\displaystyle\leq\,\sup_{y^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbb{P}}_{y^{\prime}}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathcal{M}})>\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\,]\,+\,\sup_{z\in{\mathcal{M}}}{\mathbb{P}}_{z}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{E}}^{c}_{1})\leq\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\,]\;.

It remains to find a time-scale
\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}
which turns
the previous two expressions negligible.

By Lemma
4.2
and Chebyshev inequality, the first one tends to

0
as
\epsilon\to 0
provided

\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\succ\epsilon^{-1}
. By Lemma
4.4
,
the second term vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
provided

\vartheta^{(1)}_{\epsilon}\leq e^{h/\epsilon}
for some
h<{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
. This completes the proof of (
7.5
) for
p=1
.

Fix
2\leq p\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
, and assume that (
7.5
) holds for
p-1
. In
this case, as
\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\succ\theta^{(p-1)}_{\epsilon}
,

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\sup_{t\geq c}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t)\notin{\mathscr{E}}_{p-1}\,]\;=\;0\;.

It remains to show that

\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\,\sup_{t\geq c}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}\,{\mathbb{P}}_{x}^{p,\epsilon}[\,{\mathtt{x}}(t)\in{\mathscr{E}}_{p-1}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}_{p}\,]\;=\;0\;.

(7.6)

According to the first part of the proof, it is enough to find a
time-scale
\vartheta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\prec\theta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}
such
that

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{y^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathbb{P}}_{y^{\prime}}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p})>\vartheta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,]\,=\,0\,,\quad\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{z\in{\mathscr{M}}_{p}}{\mathbb{P}}_{z}^{\epsilon}[\,\tau({\mathscr{E}}_{p-1}\setminus{\mathscr{E}}_{p})\leq\vartheta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\,]\,=\,0\;,

where, recall from (
2.13
),

{\mathscr{M}}_{p}=\cup_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k)
. By Corollary
4.7
and
Lemma
4.3
, these assertions holds provided

\epsilon^{-1}\,e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}/\epsilon}\prec\vartheta^{(p)}_{\epsilon}\prec e^{({\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}+\kappa_{0})/\epsilon}
, where
\kappa_{0}
is the postive constant introduced in Corollary

4.7
. This completes the proof of (
7.6
) and the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2

Fix
{\mathfrak{n}}\geq 1
,
0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
,

k_{1},\dots,k_{{\mathfrak{n}}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Fix a time-scale

\vartheta_{\epsilon}
such that

\epsilon^{-1}e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{p-1}/\epsilon}\prec\vartheta_{\epsilon}\prec e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}/\epsilon}
. Then, by Lemma
4.3
,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathbb{P}}^{p,\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,\bigcap_{j=1}^{{\mathfrak{n}}}\big\{\,{\mathtt{x}}(t_{j})\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{j}))\,\big\}\,\Big]

(7.7)

\displaystyle=\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1})<\vartheta_{\epsilon}\,,\,\bigcap_{j=1}^{{\mathfrak{n}}}\big\{\,{\mathtt{x}}(\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}t_{j})\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{j}))\,\big\}\,\Big]\,+\,R^{(1)}_{\epsilon}(x)\,,

where (
7.3
) holds for
R=R^{(i)}
.

As
\vartheta_{\epsilon}\prec e^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{p}/\epsilon}
y the strong
Markov property, the first term on the right-hand side of the
penultimate displayed formula is equal to

{\mathbb{E}}^{\epsilon}_{x}\Big[\,{\mathtt{1}}\big\{\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1})<\vartheta_{\epsilon}\,\big\}\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\epsilon}_{{\mathtt{x}}(\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}))}\Big[\,\bigcap_{j=1}^{{\mathfrak{n}}}\Big\{\,{\mathtt{x}}(\theta_{\epsilon}^{(p)}t_{j}-\tau({\mathscr{M}}_{p-1}))\in{\mathscr{E}}({\mathscr{M}}_{p}(k_{j}))\,\Big\}\,\Big]\,\Big]\,.

Recall from (
2.26
) the definition of
{\mathtt{l}}_{p-1}(x)
,

{\mathtt{r}}_{p-1}(x)
. Let

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathfrak{m}}({\mathtt{l}}_{p-1}(x))=m^{+}_{p-1,{\mathtt{l}}_{p-1}(x)}
,
